It's impulsive emotional and has a negative expected value.  Excellent trading deals with incomplete information and variance of a game like Blackjack but has a positive expected value like a professional poker player at a table of weaker opponents.  1 final note here, the term gambling usually describes a form of entertainment, where the gambler's odds or expectation are negative, some forms of gambling, like poker can have a positive expectation. If the player is highly skilled, I would say that when a highly skilled and highly disciplined poker player sits down at a table full of tourists or recreational poker players that is not gambling.  The skilled player has a significant mathematical Edge. Poker is much closer to trading than most forms of gambling because it offers a small group of Highly skilled players, the ability to generate abnormal returns consistently.  In other words, poker is skill, not luck research confirms this unequivocally.  I think it's interesting that research also shows that hedge fund managers who are skilled in Texas Holdem, outperform those who are not  Here's the conclusion. Reached by  We find that hedge fund managers who do well in poker tournaments have significantly. Better fund performance. This effect is stronger for tournaments with more entrance, larger buy-ins, larger cash prizes. And for managers who win, multiple tournaments, suggesting poker skills are correlated with fund management skills like Taylor and billions. This further solidifies the skill link between poker and trading and separates them from luck-based gambling, such as roulette craps and mostly blackjack.  Trading like poker involves significant variance and path. Dependence, but long-run outcomes are primarily driven by skill.  We will talk much more about skill luck and variance in chapter 11. For now remember, if you want to gamble, go to a casino  A substantial portion of investor underperformance. Can be explained simply by the fact that investors are active when they should be doing nothing.  This is proven again and again by the consistent and sizable outperformance of index funds versus active managers and by the poor returns in retail accounts, most of the time you should be doing nothing.  this is a simple concept, but 1 that Is extremely difficult to put into practice in real life. Your default mode should always be to do nothing.  Flat is the most powerful position in trading. When you have no position, you are unbiased ready to pounce and unconstrained, by any prior view or Notions. You are not anchored on any price or level, you are most open-minded and Nimble when you have no position,  Yet people often feel bad when they're flat, especially if they remain flat for many days, I'm not putting my Capital to work. They might think or my boss is going to think I'm lazy.  Human nature equates doing nothing with laziness and in most jobs, that's a pretty accurate assessment and employee who sits there and does nothing over. Multiple days, is probably not doing a great job in most roles  In trading though, the opposite can be true. A Trader with no position, has no bias.  Now, here are some ways to combat overtrading.  1, think about your motivations for trading. Do you over trade? Why?  Self-awareness is key. Before any action can be taken to fix the problem.  2.  Create speed bumps. That must be cleared before you can trade. Maybe that means filling in a quick pre-trained checklist. So almost everyone gets busy on the proof. End quote, John Kenneth Galbraith.  There's a good chance you already know about confirmation bias. It's 1 of the best known and most discussed forms of bias out there it is most discussed because it is critically important and hard to fight even when you understand it.  Confirmation bias is the tendency for humans to seek and incorporate information that supports their pre-existing View and ignore or reject information. That does not  Instead of impartially evaluating new evidence in the search for unbiased conclusions about the market, people tend to stick to their current View and then look for evidence to support it while ignoring evidence that does not.  While Kahneman and tversky tend to be the names, we most associate with behavioral bias confirmation bias was discovered by British psychologist, Peter Watson. His experiment was simple, he gave the subjects, the series, 2, 4 6, then told them it sat, Ified his pattern rule.  He then told subjects they could construct other sets of 3 numbers to test their assumptions, about the pattern rule each time, the subject showed the experimenter, their 3 numbers, the experimenter would tell them whether or not it satisfied. The pattern rule until the subject was confident. They figured out the rule  Subjects would test, either a series like, 8 101, 12 satisfies, the rule and 22 24/26, satisfies the rule or 369, satisfies the rule and 51015 satisfies the rule or 4812 or 10 2030, they all satisfy the rule till eventually most would answer that the pattern rule is either.  Count up by twos, count up by some multiple sum formula, like add the first 2 numbers to get the third number.  But whatever, answer people came to they tended to get an idea and then test it over and over and over till they were certain it was right what they did not do. Generally was tried deliberately wrong sequences to see if those might work.  The actual pattern rule was any 3 numbers that increase in value. Anyone that tested 1 123 for example would have instantly ruled out all 3 patterns, I just listed above  But subjects were always busy, testing patterns, that fit their rule without ever thinking to test those that did not.  Instead of looking for more and more evidence that their hypothesis was correct, they should have tried sequences that did not fit their hypothesis in a test to invalidate. It look harder for evidence and arguments against your hypothesis.  Confirmation bias is 1 of the most harmful bugs in human reasoning. You see it everywhere in media and on Twitter, as consumers of information ignore competing views in augment, their existing opinions with more and more confirmatory evidence.  The concept is especially disruptive in trading and investing because it inhibits, good thinking, Access into a view and blocks out. Useful non-conforming information.  Also, other types of bias such as the endowment effect work together with confirmation bias and make it very difficult for investors to change their mind. Once they take a position in the market, you see confirmation bias in action, almost every day on a trading floor,  Example, you've been following zap, Corp for months and you bought the stock at good levels. Everyone's been excitedly waiting for the hot news zap fidget phone to come out a headline hits. Zap reports 90-day fidget phone launch delay due to supplier issues  An unbiased Market, Watcher might think that is bearish, the stock drops 4%, but you're a fan of Zap, you're long, the stock and you might think something more like, man, this isn't news phones still coming out, it's not even zaps fault. This is an overreaction. The Stock's going to bounce.  meanwhile, 2 analysts downgrade, the stock they Say, this is further evidence that zaps supply chain is overly reliant on a single chip supplier in Vietnam. And that the supplier is severely mismanaged. The stock drops, another 4%. You might think, man, I can't believe how much the Market's overreacting here,  On the other hand, bears are probably yelling. I knew this was going to happen.  How to combat confirmation bias?  1 read about confirmation bias.  It's 1 of the most catalogued psychological phenomena at their and the research is surprisingly interesting. Confirmation bias is powerful and kind of frightening because it's severely compromises the human capacity for reason.  For example, if you have 2 people who have opposite views on a divisive issue, like capital punishment and give them new information, here's what happens. This is an excerpt from an excellent article on confirmation bias from New Yorker magazine.  Quote, 1 of the most famous of these confirmation bias experiments was conducted. At Stanford for this experiment researchers. Rounded up a group of students who had opposing opinions about capital punishment, half the students were in favor of it and thought that it deterred crime, the other half were against it and thought it had no effect on crime.  The students were asked to respond to 2 studies 1 provided data in support of the deterrence argument and the other provided data that called it into question. Both studies, you guessed. It were made up and had been designed to present what were objectively speaking equally compelling statistics.  The students who had originally supported capital punishment, rated, the pro deterrence data, highly credible and the anti-drunk. Convincing the students who originally opposed capital punishment, did the reverse at the end of the experiment. The students were asked once again about their views,  those who'd started out Pro capital punishment were now even more in favor of it. Those who deposed, it were even more hostile. End quote. You have not really recognized how confirmation bias negatively impacts your decision-making.  2 actively consider other hypotheses. We talked about this in chapter 5. What might it say? While many smart, individuals are happy to play Devil's Advocate. Whenever they hear someone else's ideas, they are often unable to poke holes in their own logic. Trader bias? Number 4, extrapolation.  1 thing you learned after 5 years or so of working on Wall Street is that most for Tests. You read are simply extrapolations of the current trend.  In the PDF. I strongly recommend you read super forecasting by tetlock and Gardener to get a full grasp of The Perils of forecasting. It's 1 of the best books I've ever read.  These kind of charts are comical to me, but it's even funnier when you see it in real time.  In 2017, Emmanuel macron, won the presidency in France. And this was Seen as a bullish development, for the Euro Euro dollars, started trending higher, and every analyst set a topside Target of 115 or 120.  When euro dollar rallied to 120 fairly quickly forecasters, did not say mission accomplished. They simply raised their for to 1.25. When it got to 1:25, they raised their forecasts to 1:30.  In the middle of the chart, you can see where euro dollar peaked and you see the black line, crashes lower, which is the price of euro dollar, then the forecasts follow a few months later as Euro dollars sold off. Hard from 1 125 to 118 analysts climbed over each other to lower their forecasts for months and months and months. That's not forecasting. That's extrapolation.  The default and Easy View to take in the market and in the world is to assume that whatever is going on right now, will continue. But that is often not how the real world Works periods. About performance are very often followed by reverse. Version to the mean.  This is the logical explanation for the sophomore, Jinx, the cheer hedge, the Sports Illustrated Jinx and the magazine cover indicator.  A theme or Market move that has become such an important story that it landed on. A magazine cover has probably outperformed in a major way. And thus, is more likely to revert to the mean, than to further outperformed its already stellar outperformance.  In markets, there's another reason why price reverts to the mean extremely low or high prices change the supply, and demand profile for the asset.  If oil goes to 100 New rigs, come on board, and increase Supply. And this pushes the price lower. If oil goes to 20 people, buy more fuel guzzling trucks because gas is cheaper this increase in demand for gasoline eventually pushes oil prices back up. If the price of corn, triples companies will substitute other Commodities that are cheaper and so on  Watch for changes in supply and demand in your market after a large price change. Changes our medium or long-term sellers or buyers entering the market to take advantage of high or low prices.  This change in participant Behavior. Can mark the beginning of the end of a trend?  the number 1 input into almost every forecast is the current level and trend of the asset  There are notable exceptions like everyone mostly called for higher us yields for 40 years, even as they trended lower. But generally you will see that when something is going up, its forecasts to go up more. When something's going down, forecasters expect it to keep going down.  Extrapolation bias is a form of recency bias. Humans tend to overweight more recent information in most analysis, simply because that information is more available or top of mind.  Events from years ago are harder to remember and incorporate.  This is why you see analysts put out bullish projections for oil when it's trading at 100 and bearish predictions for oil when it's trading at 25. When Greek debt was the most attractive and yielded 35%, the ratings, agencies downgraded it. And as of December 2020 it's the least attractive. It's ever been with yields close to zero and they upgraded it.  Note that yield moves opposite to the price of the bond. So the ratings agency downgraded, the bond at the lows and upgraded it at the highs.  My points not to troll the rating agencies and I know technically, they're not forecasting. They're just now casting, the point is just that, when an asset is in trouble, everyone hates it. When an asset's ripping, everyone loves it.  And in the PDF, I also show a chart of oil and oil price forecasts. And again, you note that the price moves first and the forecast follows and has no predictive value.  Extrapolation bias does not just impact economists and forecasters it impacts Traders. Big time.  Most Traders are much more comfortable, buying things that are going up and selling things that are going down. Sometimes, this isn't appropriate strategy. Often it is not the bias manifests in another way besides the tendency to chase the market higher and lower, many Traders put on a trade. And when it hits their target, instead of taking profit, they raise their target. It looks bad, I can't take profits here. They'll say  Be aware of this bias. Notice it in your thought patterns be rational.  As my friend, the gold Crow says, it always looks great on the highs and terrible on the lows.  When there's a strong Trend and you have faith in it for multiple reasons, that's not extrapolation bias. That's a rational expectation. That the thing will go up when a stock rallies 3 bucks out of nowhere. And your instinct is to buy it because someone must know something you're making an irrational extrapolation. There are Traders out there that have the opposite bias and tend to trade mean reversion all the time. Even when things are trending, that's me. I'm More biased towards mean reversion and the bias can be just as costly as extrapolation trading mean reversion. All the time, can be like trying to catch falling pianos. Strong. Trend equals falling piano.  Do you like to catch falling pianos?  This is really important. Good Traders. You should identify and trade, what works in the current market, don't commit to 1 trading style and hope the market cooperates. It won't  Negative extrapolation.  Well, what we typically witness in financial markets is positive extrapolation. That is everyone assumes, whatever's happening will keep happening. There can also be the problem of negative Extrapolation that is it has never happened before. So it won't happen. Now this is a failure of imagination. The best Outlook is to always consider almost any possibility. No matter how unlikely.  This doesn't mean you need to position for extremely low probability outcomes, all the time. You just need to consider them.  A famous example of negative extrapolation is Ben bernanke's famous failure of imagination. Just before the 2008 Global financial crisis  Interviewer tell me, what is the worst case scenario? Sir, we have so many economists coming on our air and saying, oh this is a bubble and it's going to burst. And this is going to be a real issue for the economy. Some say, it could even cause a recession at some point. What is the worst case scenario? If, in fact, we were to see housing prices come down substantially across the country.  And Bernanke responds.  Well, I guess I don't buy your premise. It's a pretty unlikely possibility. We've never had a decline. In house prices on a nationwide basis. So what I think is more likely is that house prices will slow. May be stabilized, might slow consumption spending of it. I don't think it's going to drive the economy too far from its full employment path, though.  In case you were born very recently, housing went down nationwide in 2008 and went down a lot. People use similar logic when assessing the Swiss National Bank Euro Swiss floor at 120 before it broke. In 2015. The idea was they held it this long? There's no way they'll let it break, and it's gone.  Another concept that long seemed ridiculous and unimaginable but then quickly became mainstream is the idea of negative interest rates most market pricing before 2012 or so, always treated zero as the absolute lower Bound for interest rates.  Therefore punters made bets in Swiss rates, for example, that went horribly wrong when the Swiss National Bank cut rates below. Zero always remember anything. Can happen.  This does not mean you need to be wracked with fear on high alert for extremely low probability events. All the time, it means you need to be open-minded to outliers and their possible impact.  Consider low probability events and think about how they could influence your risk of ruin.  No matter how unlikely an event might be, it's not worth taking the other side if it can blow you up.  many learned this, the hard way, after the Swiss National Bank debacle in 2015,  Trader bias. Number 5, favorites. Versus long shots.  Human beings generally prefer to bet on long shots and avoid favorites, even though favorites almost always offer higher expected returns.  Before we delve into why that might be and how it's relevant to trading. Let's look at a bit of evidence from the world of horse racing.  Here's the money quote and an accompanying chart in the PDF from a 2010 paper. By Eric snowberg and just And woofers.  Quote.  The racetrack provides a natural laboratory for economists. Interested in understanding decision-making under uncertainty.  The most discussed empirical regularity in racetrack gambling markets, is the favorite Longshot bias, equilibrium market, prices betting odds, provide biased, estimates of the probability of a horse. Winning specifically betters value long shots, more than expected given how rarely they win and they value favorites to little given how often they win.  The figure illustrates that the rate of return to betting on horses with odds of 101 or greater is about -61 betting randomly yields averaged returns of minus 23%. While betting the favorite in every race yields losses of only 5.5%,  So betting on favorites is clearly a less bad strategy than betting on Long Shots yet. Even though the effect is well known, it's persisted over many decades Favorite long shop bias was initially discussed in 1949, and yet the effect still shows up in many but not all gambling and financial markets.  The more asymmetric and Lotto like the payout, the more likely Traders investors gamblers and regular folk will over BET The Longshots.  There are 3 main reasons for the existence of favorite Longshot bias.  1 the marginal utility of a large win is often higher than the marginal utility of a small win. Even if the odds of the small win are near 100% with a fixed amount of Capital At Risk, small payoffs, don't really move the needle, financially, or psychologically.  Say, you're a hedge fund manager who wants to generate a 10% return this year.  You're offered 2 trades. Both risking 5 basis points. If wrong.  Trade 1 has an 80% chance of making 5 basis points. So the expected value is plus 3, and trade 2 has a 5% chance of making 80 basis points, expected value of negative. Of 0.75.  Even though trade 1 is clearly more attractive, mathematically many Traders, whether they will admit it or not are more attracted to trade 2 because it moves the needle for their year-to-date p&l if it hits. Well, the first 1 does not unless they make a huge bet.  2 bettinger  And 3 human beings tend to overestimate the likelyhood of lower probability of events and underestimate those with higher probability.  Keep this in mind when assessing odds, if you think something's a 5% shot, you're probably too high. If you think something's an 85% shot, you're probably too low in contrast. Humans are good at evaluating probabilities in the middle of the Spectrum in the 25% to 60% Zone.  Since estimating probability is a critical skill in trading. You must understand, it is more More difficult to estimate high and low probabilities than those near the middle.  In trading the long shot bias. Effect shows up in language like levered bets or outstanding risk reward and the ever popular asymmetric payout profile.  Looking for asymmetric payouts, makes sense in aggregate. If you're always betting, a dollar to win 50 cents in a game where the odds are not known beforehand. Only estimated it's hard to achieve persistent success. On the other hand this preference for long shots can sometimes lead to bad trades with good updates.  The Longshot mentality is bolstered by Financial media features. That shower adulation on tail Risk Managers in the rare moments. They're highly levered Insurance bets pay off.  There are no stories written about the tail risk fund that bleeds for 4 straight years. While the vix is pinned at 12, the takeaway here is be aware of favorite Longshot bias, don't get sucked in, by bad trades with good Optics.  An example. Favorite long shot bias in action.  You often see favorite long shot bias over economic and Central Bank events. For example, when a central bank meeting is coming up and there's a tiny but not zero chance of that Central Bank, cutting rates, the market loves to go into the meeting short, the currency this often creates an imbalance where there are so many people betting on the long shot, a rate cut that you get an outsized move when no cut is delivered.  Here's an example in early February 2020 Co 19 was popping up all over the world and had triggered the first wave of fear about the global economy given Australia's dependence on Commodities and Global growth. Some observers thought the Reserve Bank of Australia might cut rates in response to the virus Contagion.  Interest rate, markets price, the probability of an interest rate, cut at less than 10%, but if the cut was delivered, Aussie dollar might drop 100 points.  the simple Longshot trade was to go short Aussie and cover quickly if the RBA did not Announcer rate, cut.  Traders in this setup. Thought they were risking a few Pips for a chance to make a 100 Pips of profit.  But in the PDF, I show a chart of what happened and the RBA left rates unchanged and Aussie spiked much more than expected.  There were so many Traders, caught short on the lotto ticket trade, the pair Gap, 30 Pips higher from 66.90 to 6720.  Traders thought they were risking 5 Pips to make a 100 but they were actually risking 30 to make a 100  So if you assigned a 10% chance to the RBA cut, which was probably generous the expected value of the trade, with 5 Pips of downside was plus 5.5, but in real life, because the know cut cost you 30 points. The expected value of the trade was actually minus 17 Pips and there's a table in the PDF that explains all that math note that while we didn't know for sure because it didn't happen, a cut from the RBA, might have yielded a drop of less than 100 points because the position was so Started. In other words, the expected value of the trade might have even been worse than minus 17 basis points.  Anytime you hear people talking about a lotto ticket trade or a trade, where there's very little risk or where you win, in almost every scenario be cautious. This is probably a sign that Traders are flocking to a trade that has great Optics on paper, but possibly bad mathematics in real life. Those trades get crowded as a trade, gets crowded, the risk reward gets worse and your ability to get out. Quickly becomes impaired.  Favorite long shot bias also shows up in option. Pricing, speculators love to buy low. Delta options, that is cheap options that pay out a huge windfall, if something very unlikely happens  And market makers generally do not like selling them. Therefore research shows that the lower the probability of an option paying off and thus the more leverage is embedded in that option. The more overpriced it will tend to be  Watch for favorite Longshot, bias in your own trading and in setups that. Unfold in front of you.  Chapter 7 continued.  Trader bias. Number 6, round numbers.  When you fill up your car with gas? Do you sometimes have the urge to round it off to the nearest Dollar? Even though you're paying with a credit card?  Do you get a tiny thrill watching? A car's odometer. Roll over from 99999 to 100,000.  For many amateur. Marathon runners a finishing time of 3 hours, 59 minutes and 58 seconds is a great success. Well, 4 hours and 2 seconds is a disappointment. These are examples of round number bias, human beings tend to pay attention to round numbers and treat them as special or more important than other numbers.  Okay sure, Brent. But who cares?  You should.  round number bias, appears in financial markets, and while the knowledge will not be Life or career changing for you, it will save you from the occasional big disappointment.  Due to round number bias, many Market, participants leave their orders on the round numbers. Many more than you would see if orders were randomly or evenly distributed.  In the PDF are 2 examples from the world of equities, but these anomalies are present in every Market. I've studied  The 2 charts show the distribution of the last 2, digits, the sense of each daily high and low in Tesla and Netflix from 2015 to 2020.  For example, 52040 would be 40. 765, exactly, would be 000 509.33 would be 33, Etc.  And in the charts, you can see a huge over representation of highs and lows at 0000 and 50. Exactly. As round number bias would predict  therefore, if you leave an order to buy at say 400, 731 your statistically more likely to get filled than the person with an order at 4 7 3. 9 9.  Most days, you'll probably get filled at any 1 of those 3 levels.  But the occasional time you get filled at 01, when you would not have got filled once at lower can have a big impact on your performance.  On the flip side. If you're leaving, stop loss, orders, always leave them on the correct side of the round number. Sell stops should be below, 000 and buy stops, should be above the day. You leave a stop at 474.99 and the low is 475. Exactly, you'll feel like you got lucky but these tiny alterations in Behavior can sometimes be skill masquerading as good luck.  And when that happens, you owe me a beer.  Trader bias. Number 7, epifania. Quote chaos is not dangerous until it begins to look orderly end quote. Max Gunther the Zurich axioms.  Great Traders are masters of pattern recognition. They'll see a setup and recognize it either consciously or unconsciously? I remember many times after great runs of trading that I felt like I knew what was going to happen before it happened. The ability comes from the incredible powers of human pattern recognition.  Problem is humans are built to see patterns even when none exists.  apophenia or patternicity are terms psychologists use to describe the human predisposition to see patterns or connections, even where none exists  epifania leads to many flaws in human thinking and explains why people see Jesus on toast, UFOs in the sky Bigfoot in the woods. And so on the human bias toward pattern, recognition also leads gamblers, who are on a winning streak to believe they will keep winning even Smart logical. People often feel like they're heating up after a lucky series of hands at a poker table.  This is also known as clustering illusion. People have a hard time believing that streaks are the result of Randomness even though random data contains many streaks,  We're wired to find meaning and significance in random and meaningless data.  Apophenia goes a long way toward explaining, why humans believe conspiracy, theories, suffer from paranoia and embrace pseudo science. When you see patterns everywhere, you see me meaning and connections too.  Understand that you are not immune, you are predisposed to see patterns, even where none exist.  The human ability to see patterns is a critical part of our Evolution and allows us to understand the world without having to analyze every single new piece of information.  If we've never seen a large black cat with large teeth on the Savannah, a useful evolutionary trait was to assume that Its Behavior and risk profile is similar. To other large animals with huge teeth, we've encountered in the past.  We can correctly infer that the presence of dark clouds in the sky above us represents an increased probability of rain in the next few hours. We know, hissing sound. Nearby means snake bite risk is higher than normal and we dramatically increase our alert level.  Evolution favored false positives, over false negatives because the cost of a false positive, in the wild was much lower than a false negative for example.  There's a loud grumbling noise in the bush caveman core cannot clearly identify it.  False positive type 1 error. Hmm. That sounded like a lion. I think I'm out of here.  Cost grow may have used some unnecessary energy, avoiding what could have been just a random sound?  Or false negative type 2 error.  Hmm. I wonder what that grumbly noise was? Didn't sound exactly like a lion? I guess. I'm good.  42 seconds later. Oh shit, it was a lot.  Cost C is dead.  Seeing patterns where there are? None may be superior evolutionary Behavior, but it's not helpful in trading given the very strong human instinct to see patterns how useful do you think simple eyeballing of charts basic trend lines and breakout patterns is likely to be what are the odds that chart break is just a standard false positive? Like the loud noise in the bush. Now add on human confirmation bias. Are you just seeing what you want to see? Probably  This does not mean, there are no patterns in the world. If you run into heavy traffic, 4 days in a row, at a pretty clear intersection. It makes sense to consider. Whether there's causation investigating further or temporarily changing your route is likely to be up optimal.  But you need to be aware. That the way humans see patterns is biased to type 1 errors. You need to be discriminating and use a tight filter. When looking for patterns, you need to consider that what you are looking at could be right. Random.  How to combat apophenia.  There are 3 ways to beat apophenia in trading awareness, skepticism and Analysis.  1 awareness.  Hopefully this section has you thinking about and more aware of apophenia and patternicity. It's a deeply ingrained and basic part of human cognitive function, once you appreciate that, the human brain is biased toward pattern, recognition, new patterns, you detect are placed in the proper context.  2.  Start from a place of skepticism, when you see a pattern or a correlation assume, it's bogus and try to work out why that might be wrong, most people assume patterns are valid. And then spend time further validating them with other analysis, or information, you should start by assuming patterns are meaningless and work your way towards meaning via analysis.  And that's 3 analysis. Let's say you see a headline on Bloomberg, death cross in silver. Futures triggers bear. Signal you, pull up the story and see a fugly chart where the 50-day moving average has just crossed below. The 200-day, is your first instinct to sell silver or to investigate more deeply patterns? Like, this should not be taken at face value. Most of them are meaningless and have no empirical basis, take half an hour to pull some data into Excel and back test the pattern.  Does it have any predictive value?  Now, when I say patterns that does not just mean technical analysis, it's any cause and effect relationship that appears to exist in markets. Again, humans tend to ascribe causality to many things that are the result of pure chance.  On the other hand patterns, like the following examples could be meaningful persistent and predictive.  Gold tends to go lower from 9:00 a.m. to 10:00 a.m. maybe gold producers sell at this time.  Whenever us rates, go lower Delian goes lower Japanese investors prefer higher rates.  Apple tends to lead qqqq. large caps lead, indexes, and so on sophisticated pattern recognition can be a significant source of edge when it's free from bias,  I spend a good part of my time analyzing data in an attempt to determine whether patterns have predictive value or not.  Most often the answer is not.  Taking the time to analyze patterns and to determine whether or not they're useful for forecasting is a good way to overcome epifania and discover useful tools for beating the market.  This is my simple Force step method for accepting or rejecting patterns.  1 identify a pattern or relationship.  2 generate a hypothesis. The best patterns have some sort of, underlying logic. If gold goes down every day from 9 a.m. to 10:00 a.m. maybe that's the window. When gold producers sell, if you can logically explain why a pattern works, it's much more likely to be non-random and persistent.  3, analyze the data 4 except, or dismiss the Pattern.  Anyone who took a science class as a kid should recognize this. As a short form version of the scientific method, most patterns, you analyze will be worthless, you'll dismiss them and move on. On the other hand, the few patterns you find that are, non-random, can be incredibly valuable to your future Trading.  Every time you notice a pattern reduce it to something, you can test and then get to work, you don't need sophisticated quantitative skills to do basic back testing of simple theories.  Once you back test a pattern, you have a much deeper understanding of whether or not it's random and, you know, whether to factor it into your trading decisions. Back testing is an entire field of quantitative research and there's considerable complexity involved. I'm not a computational finance or applied math expert. You come up with  when testing your hypothesis, be aware of a few things 1, the law of small numbers,  You need a decent sample of observations, for your analysis, to mean anything. If you see that Tesla stock went down the last 4, aprils, in a row, that's meaningless. If it went down, 33 of the last 41, Fridays, that might mean something.  Well, there's no hard rule for legit sample size, any study, using less than 30 observations is risky and likely to be unreliable.  2, the harder you have to look the more likely, whatever you find is bogus or biased.  Let's say you want to see how the stock market performs the Monday after a 3% drop on Friday you find nothing. Interesting. So then you test how it performs after a 4% drop on Friday, still nothing.  Then you up the threshold to 5% and find the subsequent Monday. A Is a 2% fall.  Then you test what if stocks fall 2% or more Thursday and 5% or more on Friday?  The more you mess around with your parameters, the more likely you are to find quote, interesting results. This is called P hacking in the research business, you keep tweaking the inputs till you get an interesting result. That is not good research.  Don't torture your data in an effort to make it confess. Keep your analysis simple.  For the best ever. And super simple explanation of how P hacking snooping and data mining work. As a quick detour, here's a bit more info on turnaround, Tuesday, in case you're not familiar with its amazing power.  Turnaround Tuesday.  During periods of Market fear, there is a common intake pattern that stock markets. Often follow markets, don't always follow this pattern, but they follow it, a surprising proportion of the time.  Thursday. Hmm, I'm getting a bit nervous here. Maybe I should reduce a bit of my stock market, exposure. Friday morning. I need to hedge. This looks really bad Friday afternoon better. Sell some S&P features. To hedge my weekend risk. Wow, what a brutal close Saturday. Everyone reads the alarming media. Of the scary things that knock the market down Friday.  Sunday, Asia and Monday morning markets Gap, open lower and selling builds as Asia, selling Cascades into Europe, New York Traders, press their shorts, Monday around 4 pm, the low Tuesday turnaround Tuesday as shorts. Get squeezed, Wednesday seesaw. Battle between bulls and bears and Thursday. Hmm, I'm getting a bit nervous here. Maybe I should reduce a bit of my stock market exposure.  The most reliable part of this pattern is turnaround, Tuesday. This is the tendency for stocks to rip higher on Tuesday. If they sold off the Friday and Monday before, it's a simple human pattern that occurs because when the news appears bad Traders, get nervous into the weekend and sell some of their Holdings on Friday.  Then they read all kinds of negative media reports about the big scary thing, and that scares them into selling more on Monday.  Investors selling pulls in momentum traders, who go short on Monday? This adds to the selling pressure Then Tuesday comes and there's nobody left to sell.  Then the shorts, get squeezed and that triggers turnaround Tuesday.  In case you think the sound silly, have a look at the evidence? In contrast, if you study all days after a 5% 1 day, drop the average return. The next day is just plus 0.9%, not terrible, but nothing amazing. Number 8, anchoring. I've always found anchoring bias to be 1 of the most interesting forms of Trader bias. The basic idea of anchoring bias is that when we are asked to guess or predict something we're biased toward the first reference point offered  Outside of Finance. This is most commonly seen in negotiation. If we're told the price of a car is 12,000, anything below that feels cheap and anything above feels expensive. Even if we find out later that the car is only worth 10,000. Will still find ourselves anchored, on the initial price heaps of Search proves this, weirdly anchoring works even if the initial number is unrelated to the question at hand.  A simple anchoring experiment which my son did for science. Fair once and it worked goes as follows subjects. First draw a random number from 1 to 100 out of a bag. Then they're asked to guess, for example,  How many African countries are in the United Nations?  Those that Drew low numbers from the bag answer consistently lower than those who drew higher numbers out of the bag.  Another famous example of anchoring is when you ask people to quickly estimate 1 * 2, * 3, * 4, * 5, * 6, * 7 * 8. You get a much lower answer than if you asked them to quickly estimate. 8, * 7, * 6, * 5 * 4, * 3, * 2 * 1.  In negotiation, you often hear never make the first offer because you're giving up too much information and optionality.  in contrast and effective, negotiation strategy can be for 1 side to make an Outrageous, first offer there by anchoring the negotiation on a figure that's hard to get away from say, your negotiating with an employer, you want to get paid thousand dollars a year and you believe you have decent power in the negotiation.  You open by suggesting a salary of 650,000. Now, both sides are anchored. So high that, when you finally settle on 550,000, everyone feels like a winner.  Once you know about anchoring, you see it all over the place, especially in how companies price their goods treadmill was 1,299. Now 9.99,  That sounds like a good deal but it could well be. The company has never sold a single unit for 1,299 and they're just trying to Anchor. You on that inflated price to make you feel good about paying 9.99.  In markets anchoring bias, manifests in 4 main ways.  1, we anchor on our entry point.  This is the most common and Powerful way you see anchoring by In markets, if you buy a stock at $99.40, that specific level becomes very powerful in your mind, you'll find it. Much easier for example to sell at 99.41 small profit than 99.39 a small loss, even though the economics of selling at these 2, different levels is practically identical.  Losing is painful and negative winning is pleasurable and positive. So even though, 2 senses, meaningless to your pnl, the difference between selling below, or above your cost, is the difference between winning and losing,  Human beings are hardwired to avoid losses as discussed earlier. Research shows the pain, we feel from losses is about double the pleasure we get from gains of equal size. This bias is called loss aversion.  A related bias is the disposition effect. This is the strong and consistent tendency for investors to prefer to sell assets that have increased in value. While holding on to assets that show a  If it goes up, you sell it. If it goes down, you hold on to it and pray for a bounce.  These related forms of bias all come down to the same thing. Humans don't like to lose. The remedy is simple. You must learn to love small losses.  When you take a small loss, try to feel good about it, say taking small losses is the way to achieve trading success. Say it over and over taking small losses, is the way to achieve trading, success taking small losses is the way to achieve trading, success, taking small losses is the way to achieve trading success.  Trading losses are an expense incurred by your trading business. Your goal is to minimize trading loss expense while maximizing trading Revenue. You minimize trading loss expense by cutting bad trades quickly and by sticking to your plan, you increase trading loss expense with shoddy risk management, poor discipline and sloppy Trading.  as a beginning Trader, you will no doubt hear your Yourself thinking this trade feels wrong as soon as I get to break, even I'm out.  2 problems here. Problem 1. This trade feels wrong, is not rational unpack. Why it feels wrong? Is there something inherently wrong with the price action? Or is the stock just not moving and you're bored? Those are 2 completely different things. Often your instincts are just your impatience and emotions trying to sabotage a perfectly good trade.  Problem 2. As soon as I get to break, even I'm out is not logical. Do you think the market cares where you got in as if there's something magical about your entry point, if you've determined biologic that the trade is not worth holding on to get out. If you've not determined you should get out of the trade, maintain your existing parameters.  Every trade you put on should have a stop loss and a take profit after that. You need a solid reason to cut the trade. Otherwise stick to your original plan. Don't keep recalibrating based on every emotion. You feel motions are fine. But overreacting to them is not.  Just remember your entry point is meaningless.  2. We tend to anchor on price level highs and lows. The feeling you get from the market is often more about recent changes, not absolute moves. This is why if you buy gold at 1570 and it goes to 1670, you feel good. But then when it drops to 1600, you feel more bad than good even though you're still $30 in the money.  How we feel about a trade is often more influenced by recent changes in price than by aggregate moves.  Say, your long gold with a stop at 1540 and I take profit at 1680. It rallies to 16770, then pulls back to 1620.  Quite often a Trader will say if it just gets back to 16770, I'll get out.  They are anchoring on the high because that was their moment of Maximum pleasure. Sure, if 1670 is a major tech level now, and you realize 1680 was the wrong level to take profit, be my guest and move your tail. Profit lower but don't just move it because you're anchored on that level now.  3.  We also anchor on p&l highs, have you ever been up 15,000 at midday then you give back half and you say, okay? If I just get back to 15,000 I'll square up. That's not rational when you hear your internal monologue saying, something like this recognize that, you're not being rational trade, the current setup, not the arbitrary. Pnl threshold, you hit 2 hours ago  1 of the key overall lessons. I hope you get from this book. Is this recognize when you are thinking or behaving irrationally and stop it,  even after 25 years of trading, I still think and say irrational things on the trading floor.  The biggest difference now is that I hear myself saying these things and quietly laugh at myself, the ability to Get outside yourself and see when you are not being, rational is a necessary step towards becoming an alpha Trader.  4.  Economists, anchor on prior. Economic data, if the non-farm payrolls number NFP was plus 325,000 last month and all signs point to a much lower. Number this month, research shows that economists will tend to anchor on the prior number and their forecasts on average will be too high.  Say you're an economist and you're extremely detailed and complex. Econometric model says that this month NFP should be 38,000 if last month was 325,000 that 38,000 number feels kind of low.  You'll be inclined to submit a number. That sounds a bit less extreme or more irrational in quotes like plus 100,000. In fact that number is less rational and is influenced by anchoring bias.  if you trade the economic data look for moments, when this month's data is expected to be significantly different from last month's, then assume the actual outcome will show an even larger discrepancy than economists forecast, suggests  if you're interested in the specific topic, Of how economists. Anchor check out Campbell and Sharp's paper. Here's a quick excerpt.  We examine whether expert, consensus forecasts a monthly economic releases from 1990 to 2006, have a tendency to be systematically biased, toward the value of previous months, data releases. We find broad-based and significant evidence for the anchoring hypothesis. Consensus forecasts are biased towards the values of previous months data, releases, which in some cases results in sizable, predictable forecast, errors.  Trader bias. Number 9 hurting.  1 great. Paradox of markets is that they are mostly driven by the wisdom of the crowd but occasionally by its Madness separating. The 2 is a key source of alpha before we talk about hurting and temporary Madness in markets. Let's first discuss the wisdom of crowds.  Most of the time the errors made by buyers and sellers cancel out. And the average of all the transactions is some approximation of a reasonable equilibrium price. This outcome is similar to the jelly bean experiment in that experiment. A crowd of people are asked to guess how many jelly beans are in a jar. The interesting result is that the crowd the average of all the guesses is always very accurate and often more accurate than any individual in the entire crowd.  This is astounding and suggests. You should be humble when trying to beat the market.  Instead of a bunch of undergrads guessing. How many jelly beans are in the jar. Imagine a thousand. Highly trained quants who have millions of dollars to invest in Jellybean estimation technology. Do you think you can beat them? That's a more apt analogy for the markets. The wisdom of crowds, though, relies on diverse, and unbiased opinion and it relies on incentives. If there's no prize, estimates will be lazy, and less accurate.  or for example, if the jar is extremely far away from the people trying to estimate the number, Of jelly beans say at the very front of a huge lecture hall. The crowdsourced conclusion might be too low because the jar looks smaller from afar. If everyone is drunk or distracted, the estimate also might not be as good.  In trading. If you can explain exactly why you think the crowd is wrong. You're on the right track. Usually this would be other because the crowd is biased or the crowd's. Incentives are not aligned with the goal of finding the equilibrium price. I'll explain both in the section that follows the main. Takeaway for this section is  Assumed the crowd is wise most of the time and look for moments. When you can explain exactly why it is wrong or identify that it has gone mad.  As the amount of quality data available to all investors approaches Infinity, it is more and more difficult to find an informational Edge before 2000 or so. Economic and financial Market data were expensive and difficult to acquire for non-institutional investors as recently as 2000. 06 or 2007, a Trader with live price feeds and a solid understanding of correlation had an informational Edge over other short-term Traders.  Live news, feeds were expensive and Out Of Reach for individual investors until recently.  Now literally anyone can access reams of financial data, read dozens of well-informed blogs doing sophisticated cross market analysis, and get free, real-time news from Twitter and other sources.  Informational Edge is incredibly difficult to find this puts a premium on other types of market analysis. And I believe, potentially increases the value of sentiment, and positioning analysis.  By their nature, markets are prone to periods of hurting narrative, driven markets with many Traders, chasing the same themes and responding to the exact same information. And incentives, can drive everyone toward the same cliff.  If you can identify the times when the entire Market is running in the same direction, you can pick It and profit by going the other way. There are multiple reasons Market participants heard to the same themes and trends  1 hering is human nature. In chapter 5, we talked about the asch Conformity experiments where even when it was obvious that a line was the same size as the other lines. Many subjects would go with the crowd. If the majority said 1 line was longer.  Ash did another famous experiment to show how human beings prefer to conform with their peers.  The experiment involved, multiple actors who enter an elevator and face the back of the elevator away from the door.  Ash found that most random strangers who entered the elevator afterwards. Join the crowd and faced away from the door Conformity and hurting our basic human instincts, that help Society function. If you see a group of people acting a certain way, it sends you a strong subconscious signal, that the group behavior is appropriate and so it requires strong conscience, push back for you to act independently.  2, it's much easier. To believe in and respond to a narrative than it is to Value securities.  The internet's going to be huge. You got to buy. Cisco is an easier story to understand than Cisco is overvalued at 100 times sales.  Since human Traders are more stimulated by stories than they are by data. The price level of an asset is often less important than the story. Being told, this is why good companies can be terrible Investments.  While there's usually great uncertainty surrounding the appropriate value of a company's stock. There's frequently little disagreement over good stories.  Amazon is a revolutionary company that offers an unprecedented. Customer experience is hard to debate and easy to invest. In since most people are trading narratives, whether they want to admit it or not and narratives are often pretty clear and 1 sided investors heard into the great stories, this is not just true with stocks. It's true with macroeconomic stories, too.  Number 3, fomo. Period when aonics and Bank of Japan easing were driving dollar Yen higher portfolio. This is fomo and favorite long shop bias, all wrapped into 1 negative EV trade idea.  PMS were thinking more about the lotto ticket style payoff and how they might look bad for. Missing it. Instead of rationally sizing up, the risk reward of playing for a low probability event that was already heavily subscribed.  4 as a rule, human beings are not very original. You follow the crowds stick to Benchmark and hope nobody finds you out.  5 incentive.  Many Traders and investors are incentivized to stick with the crowd.  As an investment manager, if you hug your benchmark and own, mostly technology, stocks during a Tec bull market. You're unlikely to get fired on the other hand. If you think Tech is overvalued and you, stay underweight through the bull market, you are very likely to get fired. If tech crashes and you're long with everyone else. Your investors are likely to give you a pass.  Everyone else got killed in that crash too, man. Nobody could have seen it coming.  Traders and investors who have mortgages and kids and private school often think more about keeping their jobs than they do about maximizing profits.  Let's go back to the Jelly Bean game again for a minute. Imagine a game where 400 students are asked to guess how many jelly beans are in a jar. But the 5 high, highest and 5, lowest guesses are expelled from school and the rest compete for a prize of $50. How would that change the distribution of the guesses?  My bet is that particular set of incentives would heard everyone toward the average and greatly reduce the number of extreme guesses.  Hurting does not just happen in investing, it's a feature of trading too. Let's say you're a Trader at a macro hedge fund and there's a huge macro theme. For example, the ECB is doing quantitative easing, most people in the business are doing the trade. It often feels riskier for a Trader to go against the herd than it is for him to just follow along. Doing the opposite of what everyone else is doing, is hard, it goes directly against human nature. Most people react, strongly, to incentives. And so instead of solving for how can I make my investors? The most money they solve for what will give me the best chance of keeping my job. Even if they're not doing this consciously there's a good chance. Their subconscious is busy. Doing it for them.  To recap wisdom of the crowds works when the crowd is diverse unbiased and motivated by aligned incentives. When you see a market where these conditions are not met, start to think about going the other way,  Hurting is a tricky concept because being part of the herd is often profitable. That's always trying to be smarter than everyone else. And who's always going the other way. Humans are social animals that find comfort from doing what others do and success in trading. Requires you to go against that? Instinct somewhat regularly. So successful Traders are going to feel uncomfortable. A fair amount of the time, get used to feeling uncomfortable.  3, study and quantify sentiment sentiment data is widely available and comes in all shapes and sizes will tuck sentiment in chapter 10, whatever asset you trade investigate, the best ways to collect and analyze sentiment data.  While it's great. To listen to the market noise, and try to come up with your own view on sentiment data. Usually beats, anecdote, this is especially true in our current world where people on Twitter are constantly calling for the end of the world and Bitcoin to 1 million and so on.  Data is much more useful than randomly collected Snippets.  4 use overbought and oversold indicators. My experience with overbought and oversold indicators is that your threshold for putting on trades, should be as Extreme as possible. I like signals that come up a few times per year, such as daily sentiment index below, 10, or above 90. The reason is that sentiment is not extreme. Most of the time sentiment that is rather bullish or kind of bearish means nothing. The market will spend most of its time in the space where sentiment has no predictive value.  In fact, sentiment is a go with most of the time. The only time it's contrarian is that the mega extremes, but if you can identify these I get extremes. You can hit some huge winners.  5 always be on high alert for turns and sentiment. If you ever notice bullish sentiment at the low or bearish sentiment, at the high pay attention, generally sentiment almost always follows price.  If something's going up, people will be bullish. If prices falling, most people will be bearish, there are exceptions. Many are always Bears the stock market even when it's at the highs. But in general that's how sentiment works. Very rarely though you will see the opposite and those moments are meaningful. A prime example is the US dollar in March 2020. The co 19 pandemic triggered a liquidity crisis and there was a mad Scramble for dollars. The great dollar shortage drove euro dollar from 1113 to 108 in very short order that is stronger dollar weaker Euro.  Then the FED went all in announcing a massive QE and dollar liquidity program due to ongoing pressures in emerging. markets and equities though, the dollar remained in demand,  At that time, you could see a subtle but important change in sentiment happening.  The market which had been raging bullish, dollar started to think about the future impact of all this new dollar liquidity and sentiment slowly turned from bullish dollar to modestly bearish dollar. But the dollar was still at the ding-dong highs. This is a rare but extremely high expected value set up. If you notice a subtle shift away from a popular narrative, but the price hasn't moved yet, get ready to go. The other way often price will lag sentiment a market that's bullish on the lows or bearish. On the highs is getting ready to turn.  6 State objective.  Understand the bias of the media. You consume and look for unbiased sources of data.  Twitter liens bare stocks and bullish Bitcoin and bullish gold CNBC is almost always bullish, stocks. Zero Hedge is very bearish Wall Street. And bullish gold, whatever you read, always consider which way it leans.  I prefer to read analysts who are flexible because those who constantly stick to the same view, tend to be locked in and plagued by confirmation bias.  1 of the best financial Market Guru business models is to pick a view. Hopefully a popular 1 and pound the table on it year after year, you attract a following of like-minded people and you deliver the echo chamber that they need to reinforce their views and they will pay you for it.  This is not analysis or forecasting, its marketing. Be aware when you receive information from people like this, they will never change their View. And as such their view is probably not worth much.  Good analysts will change their View at least. Once every few years, good marketers will stick to 1 View and beat it until they have paid off the summer house in East Hampton.  Don't go on Twitter and see, 400 Bears, stock market posts and conclude the market is short. It might be Might not be.  Collecting anecdotal information, can be extremely useful, but you need to know the bias of the source.  Data is almost always better than anecdotes. So, look for objective sources of sentiment and positioning data, and focus more on those than what you consumed via traditional or New Media.  Be rational and be flexible.  Before we wrap up this section on Trader bias, let me give you a few blurbs on other important forms of bias to watch out for.  Outcome by us. This is our tendency to evaluate a decision based on the outcome and the process that was followed to reach that decision.  I discussed process versus outcome in chapter 5 and it's extremely important to separate the 2.  If you do a ton of research, come up with a great trade idea and then a totally random news event stops, you out 10 minutes after you put on the trade. That's good to decision. Bad outcome, don't worry about it.  Any Duke's book thinking in bets? Spends a ton of time on outcome bias. She calls it. Resulting, it's a great book if you want to learn more about thinking about thinking,  Squirrel. Chasing bias.  Human attention is drawn to shiny objects Homer. Simpson is often distracted by squirrels  prices on the screen are stimulating and interesting and human beings tend to confirm much more meaning to price movement than it generally deserves  Chasing bias is the tendency to enter trades in a security simply because it's moving.  Something rallies and you want to buy it, something sells off, you want to sell it, rid yourself of this bias, it's a weakness that plagues many Traders even hedge fund professionals.  It is essentially a combination of activity bias, The Need to Be Active, even when doing nothing would be a better choice and extrapolation bias, it went down, so it's going to keep going down.  Bad Traders. See price moving on the screen and think somebody knows something good. Traders know, 97% of all price action is noise.  Apple's gapping lower. Somebody must know something. Nope. No, no, nope. They probably don't there's an endless series of buys and sells going through the market and the overwhelming majority of those buys and sells have no particular Edge or informational Advantage. Could be a guy selling to pay for his divorce. It could be an asset manager reducing risk. Before he goes on vacation.  Could be tax loss selling that has nothing to do with the company. In question, don't assume that someone knows something when you see price moving,  if you're bearish Apple for 6, different reasons, and you've been watching a key level all week and apple, finally, breaks down through that level, then, by all means. So but don't be the traitor that just jumps on whatever's moving just because it's moving. If you find yourself doing that, stop, that's what bad Traders do.  That is what Traders with no plan do it is a trading equivalent of home. Take in real time.  Hindsight bias is also a constant feature of financial media. When you see a headline stocks rally on higher oil price, remember that journalists is riding with the benefit of hindsight. If you went back to the start of the day and asked people what stocks would do, if oil prices went up, you might get 50% saying, stocks would go down and 50% saying stocks would go up.  Journalists are storytellers, not Traders or financial professionals. They are always trying to make up stories to explain moves that are often driven by non- macro factors like flow and positioning.  There are many excellent Finance journalists, but there's also, the obligation for the financial media to tell a story based on fundamentals and macro when often that is not the driver of a market move.  You'll never see a headline like dollar Canada rallies as macro portfolio manager stops out of huge short because he's going on vacation.  If this is the reason dollar CAD rallies journalists will fabricate some sort. Of narrative that seems to fit in hindsight. That's their job.  Overall, you should be extremely skeptical of most articles in the financial press. They are not intentionally false, but they are biased almost exclusively to trying to fit a macroeconomic explanation. Even when short-term moves in markets, are often explained by other factors.  Don't talk about bad beats or ideas, you never put on or the times you get stopped out at the high or low. That's all just part of trading and nobody will find you interesting. If you tell them about these types of incidents always look forward,  Grizzly bias.  Compulsive bearishness is a bias that has not received as much coverage as other forms of Trader bias. The gamblers fallacy and the hot hand.  The gambler's fallacy in the hot hand are 2 opposite but related forms of bias. Some people see a roulette wheel that comes up red 8 times in a row and they think black is due  Some win a few roles at the craps table and want to increase their bet size because they have the hot hand.  Thing is in randomly generated data. There are many streaks, they don't mean anything. They're just how random data sequences go 7, heads in a row does not impact the eighth flip. The coin has no memory. Probability has no memory.  There's no Supernatural element to probability. It's a mathematical construct. The intuition people get at casino tables is nonsense. If you had a hunch you were going to win and then you won. It's a coincidence. Every spin of a roulette wheel is mutually exclusive and each role of the dice in craps is independent of the last  In finance, things are trickier. If Tesla's up 10 days in a row, is it wildly overbought, and more likely to fall on day 11? Or is day 11? An independent event with no relationship to the prior 10 can be hard to say.  The best starting point is to assume each Financial minute hour and day is independent, from the last then be open to exceptions. A good example of this is seasonality  If Sterling is up, 8, April in a row does that mean it's more likely to go up in April of year? 9, probably not, there are 10 or 15 actively traded. Currency pairs in 12 months and every year. So, odds are high that if you look hard enough, you'll find some months that show a seasonal pattern, especially if your sample size is just 8 years, but this seasonal pattern is, probably simply a random streak. There are however, many examples of persistent non-random, seasonality and price patterns the Halloween effect, more commonly known as sell in May, and go away has been in existence for more than 300 years and has worked out of sample for decades. After it was discovered. It's almost impossible to dismiss this effect as an artifact of Randomness. It's far too persistent.  If a pattern is persistent, then we try to explain it in the case of Salam and go away. The explanation could be a pattern of human optimism where we start the year, optimistic eventually overshoot. Pull back from that bit of a rational exuberance then rally in Trend or the seasonal pattern could be related to flows people. Get bonuses in January tax, refunds in April And so on, and that money goes into the stock market.  Similarly, there are micro patterns of seasonality and repetition that make sense and repeat for a reason.  The first day of the month is a day when a big lump of automatic contributions go into US employee 401K retirement, plans a good proportion of these contributions end up in index funds and as such the first day of the month tends, to be a bullish day for stocks. Same goes for the start of the year.  There are other seasonal patterns like this in various markets. And ideally, when you spot a pattern, you want to be able to ascribe some sort of logic to it. If you can't assume it's random unless you're studying a large sample of data or have some other reason to think the streak is not just a product of chance.  The paper, the hot hand belief and the gambler's fallacy in investment decisions under risk is an excellent summary of 2 ways in which investors fail to understand that most of events are independent. Here's the Upstream. Quote, we conduct experiments to analyze investment behavior in decisions under risk. Subjects can bet on the outcome of a series of coin, tosses themselves rely on randomized, Experts, or choose a risk-free alternative.  We observe that subjects who rely on the randomized experts, pick, those who are successful in the past showing Behavior consistent with the hot hand, belief obviously the term expert suffices to attract some subjects.  For those who decided on their own. We find Behavior consistent with the gambler's fallacy as the frequency of betting on heads decreases. After streaks of heads.  And quote.  Assume most events in financial markets are independent. But always look for ones that are not when you find a pattern dig deeper, keep digging until you find patterns. That might logically repeat in the future, most streaks in patterns and finance are random, but not all of them.  There are literally hundreds of ways behavioral bias in. Balances Traders and investors. I've gone through a short list of some type of bias that I believe are particularly important but there's an entire academic field and a library full of books devoted to the topic at some point. Make sure you read Thinking Fast and Slow by Kahneman, Fooled by Randomness, by TB and irrational. Exuberance by Schiller, those are my 3 favorite books on behavioral Finance also just Google behavioral, bias, trading occasionally and go down the rabbit hole of academic research and blog posts.  It is such an interesting topic and there are many people reading about it. And researching it, passionately, humans are so silly, they'll never be a shortage of foils to unpack.  Before we complete this chapter on rational thinking I want to venture into the weird and wonderful world of counterintuitive.
