This gives you 1  Note, that 1 reason, especially a chart. Based reason is never good enough for a trade idea, more appropriate would be that. You already have 3 or 4 reasons, derived, from fundamental positioning or cross market analysis and the chart symmetry gives you a nice entry point with a cheap stop-loss.  Reversal patterns.  We need to decide that you want. Want to take the other side of a trend, it's helpful to have specific indicators and patterns that will give you a signal that the time is, right?  Copious cache has been set on fire by Traders, blindly fading strong trends for no reason or using weak logic like, this is a big level or it's come too far too fast.  When I have a strong counter Trend view, I don't execute right away. Instead, I go on high alert and wait for a sign that it's time to jump in.  I would never enter a trade simply because of a reversal pattern or signal. But when I have a counter Trend view already for reasons unrelated to technical analysis. Here are 3 reversal patterns. I watch for  1 ending diagonal or Rising wedge. Now, here, I'm going to describe how it works at the highs, but these patterns work in Reverse at the lows as well.  And ending diagonal occurs when price rises in a slowly narrowing wedge as volatility contracts, even as the price continues to make new highs in the PDF, I show a nice example. Using NYX crude oil in 2018.  An interesting feature of the rising wedge formation is that as the triangle Narrows the triangle must eventually closed and the pattern must conclude.  Therefore you get a sense of when the turn might come if it's going to happen as the apex of the triangle is a timing tool.  My approach to trading wedges is simple.  When I have a counter Trend view, I wait for price to close below the wedge under the bottom line in the example of the PDF. Then I go short with a stoploss of the high.  In this example, I would sell crude at $70.80 on the day. It dropped out of the wedge on the big down day. My stop loss would be 72.85, which is above the highest high.  2 Doge a dogee is a Candlestick that forms when the opening and closing price of a security are the same.  The daily silver chart in the PDF has 2 Doge in a row after a 30% rally. The Doge are marked with large arrows,  a dogee is a Kind of indecision, price, went up and down and closed unchanged. When you see a doji or 2 Doge in a row. It's a sign that momentum has waned.  The prior trend is losing Steam.  If you are bare silver, in the case of the above chart, but wanted a sign that it was safe to get in the water. The dogee is a good signal.  Again, my methodology is simple. When I'm bearish a market that is in a strong Trend higher and I see a dogey I sell on the closed that day.  I also used OG's on hourly charts.  If I had no other information here, I would put my stop 1.5 average days, ranges away 1.5 times. The average daily range is my Baseline starting point when picking a stop-loss for a normal trade note. That I also look for major levels to consider before determining my stop loss level.  In this case, using 1 year of data as a look back, the average daily range in silver was 36 Cents. Therefore, the stop-loss is placed at the entry 1291. Plus 0.36 daily range * 1.5 so that's $13.45 is my stop loss.  There was another dogee, the next day, the high traded was 1322, and then silver cratered reversing the entire July August rally in 5 days.  Dois are simple and Powerful, warning signs of trend exhaustion.  And 3, hammers.  A hammer is a reversal candle where price makes a hard push in 1 Direction and closes at the opposite extreme. These are especially powerful if they come as a failed attempt at a major new high or low.  In the PDF. There's an example of a bull Hammer candle in Bitcoin  If you were bullish Bitcoin in early 2018 but wanted to signal to buy, you got a nice 1 in early February as the market made a bull Hammer after the 50% plus decline.  The bull Hammer is marked at the large arrow on the chart in the PDF.  the strategy is to wait for the Hammer by at the And put your stop-loss below, the low, of course, the same setup in Reverse at the highs is called a bare hammer and its bearish.  Note these patterns don't work, every time. There's also a bull hammer on that same chart in January, and that candle gave a bullish signal, that only worked for 3 days.  No technical signal or setup is going to be anywhere close to perfect. These reversal. Indicators are extremely useful though as they help you enter counter Trend trades with a set of logical risk management parameters that allow you to put on your reversal trade with meaningful Leverage.  Develop a short list of reversal indicators that you like and use them as triggers when you have a strong counter Trend view, be patient, then strike with maximum Force.  I'm now outlined a few ways to identify important levels and turning points. You should also have 1 or 2 indicators in your toolbox that help you identify the trend. And if that trend is overbought or oversold, here are 2 indicator sets. I use For Trend and overbought oversold.  Moving averages.  To measure the strength slope and direction of trend moving averages, are the simplest most effective indicator, I use 55 100 and 200 hour moving averages, on all my charts, I almost always use hourly charts for technical analysis. I sometimes zoom in with 10-minute charts or zoom out to the Daily for a different perspective. When I zoom in or out, I still use 55100 and 200 period moving averages.  Moving average are also sometimes called Mas.  Here are the 3 ways that I look at Mas 1.  The 200 hour is a great Trend indicator and I use it on its own for Trend Direction and also as support and resistance.  If I'm bearish, crude oil features and the price has been repeatedly capped by the 200 hour.  I'll use the next approach to the ma to get short, many Traders, use the 55-day in the NASDAQ as a trend indicator. If the Market's above the moving average and holding above it on pullbacks, that is a nice uptrend and vice versa, 1 of the most basic and effective strategies in trading, is to play the pullbacks in a primary Trend using a moving average. This strategy is simple and effective.  2 watch for crossovers. When the 55 our crosses up through the 200 hour that's bullish, at that point. Buying a pullback to the 55 our could be attractive if you have other good reasons to be bullish,  And 3, check the slope of the moving average. If all 3 Mas are flat. That's a choppy Range band Market. If all 3 are sloping upwards, that's a strong bull trend.  Here's a variation of a simple and effective, bullish, tactical approach, many use when they have a strong bullish view in a market.  Wait for a fast, ma to cross above a slower. Ma, which signals that an uptrend has formed. Wait for the price of the asset to retrace back to the fast. Ma buy with the stop at least. 1.5 days, ranges away, and below the 200 our ma that's simple and effective.  The selection of the period for the moving average, 55 versus 50 versus 20 is somewhat arbitrary and not super important. Just pick 2 or 3. Moving averages of different links and put them on your charts. You can also fit a moving average to the current Trend much in the way you would draw a trend line. Then if price breaks through that Ma, you know, the trend has lost its Mojo.  Relative strength indicator RSI.  Every Trader should have 1 or 2 indicators that tell them when their Market is extremely oversold or overbought on the time Horizon. They trade the Big 3 are RSI stochastics and deviation from a moving average.  RSI is the standard that everyone uses and the textbook strategy is that if it goes above 70, wait for it to fall, back below, 70 and then sell, but I use it differently.  I take as much data as possible using hourly. Data because that suits my trading time Horizon and find the Extremes in the RSI, going back. As far as I can. I then calculate the top and bottom 5%, most extreme and use these levels as get out, no matter what levels.  That is if I'm long and the RSI gets into the top 5% of the series, I cut I find that at the mega RSI extremes, the risk of a reversal is too high. So the risk reward on staying in the trend is poor.  If I'm trying to trade against the trend, I will also use these Mega extremes as an entry point.  Here's a super short excerpt from 1 of my dailies December 2nd 2020, where I use an extreme in the RSI to get short New Zealand dollar.  The textbook approach to trading RSI to wait for it to rise above 70 and then fall back below 70 and go short. I prefer to isolate the most extreme levels and use those as a reversal points right away.  I sorted all the daily New Zealand dollar, RSI, data from 2010. To know and the most extreme readings occur. When the RSI breaks above 75 that happened this week with the dramatic repricing in New Zealand rates and the non-stop flood of good vaccine news. Much goodness is in the price of New Zealand dollar now.  The table below shows how the pair performed in the 12 instances where the RSI went above 75 since 2010.  And in the PDF, I show that table.  You can see in the example, if you isolate the times when RSI is historically extreme, you can find points where you have high confidence to go the other way. When the New Zealand dollar RSI is above 75 future performance, is Raging bearish as New Zealand dollar averages a drop of 2.7% over the next 60 trading days and goes up only 25% of the time.  In this case I was already bearish risky assets and New Zealand dollar is a risky asset. So this just added to my thesis and gave me a way to play it. I went short New Zealand. At 740 with the stuff at 7111 and I take profit at 6911.  A high RSI on its own would not be enough to get me involved, but it's a nice seed that my grow into a full trade idea.  The deviation, the other overbought and oversold signal that I use, is the deviation of price, from a moving average or what, I simply call the deviation.  My technique is pretty simple. As usual, I take the price of the asset I'm trading and check how far it is from the 100 hour moving average. Then I look as far back as possible and see if the deviation tends to oscillate in a steady range or if it looks unreliable or random.  for many markets, you'll see that the deviation tends to Peak and trough around the same levels over and over when volatility is stable,  In the PDF, I show an example using smpp Minis.  This is easy to set up on your trading system, or in a spreadsheet, use historical data to determine what the extremes are in the deviation. Then use those extremes going forward as reversal signals. In this case, I filtered for the 5% of most extreme values in the deviation and this represents a move in the S&P of about 3.5%.  When using the deviation, it's important to understand the skew of your Market. Skew, is the direction of the market that tends to happen, faster and be more volatile. For example, stocks tend to sell off much more quickly than they rally. So, you might want to use a more conservative entry point for your deviation signal on the downside, or scaling gradually knowing that sell-offs can overshoot and are harder and more violent to fade.  An important note about using overbought and oversold signals think, think think think if the FED just announced a massive QE program and stocks were hired to extreme overbought on the short-term charts, that's probably not a signal that it's time to go short things that are overbought, can become more overbought and then more overbought again.  Overbought. On its own is not a reason to sell an asset. It's a warning sign that the future distribution of returns might be skewed lower, and that's it.  An elevated RSI reading or a sexy, stochastic are not reason enough to do a trade.  Incorporate overbought and oversold indicators into your process and use them to fine-tune entries and exits. But don't be the trader who Fades and moves solely, because it's come too far too fast. That is not a valid trading strategy. Remember we are using these technicals as guidance and tactical inputs and not as a systematic quantitative trading framework  Morning, quick texts, most trading jobs are busiest in the morning. Markets are already moving when you sit down, but still you need to prepare for the day by 7 a.m. each day. I'm Fielding client queries producing content planning my day. And going through hundreds of emails, I need to keep my mourning process as simple as possible. I like to create a quick sheet of text. And I put an example of it in the PDF.  Note that R1 R2 and R3 refer to the first 3 resistance levels. Well S1 S2 and S3 refer to the first 3 support levels.  The grid does not take long to update each morning because many of yesterday's levels remain valid today, and all the stuff on the bottom updates, automatically using a data feed from Bloomberg.  Good Traders. Have an established mourning process to prepare for the day. And part of that process should be to identify significant reference points for the day ahead. You know, the old saying about luck and preparation.  That concludes this section on technical analysis. I strongly advise you to keep things simple and stick with just a few indicators and techniques that make sense to you.  Remember that texts are just the frosting on the trade idea cake. Don't try to make an entire cake out of frosting.  Sentiment. And positioning.  The market cycle is always a complex Dance, featuring narrative. Sentiment sometimes the news drives the price, but quite often the price drives the news. In other words, sometimes a strong Trend takes on a life of its own and unfolds faster than the story on the ground which follows behind  While I believe sentiment, and positioning are important variables to consider in every trade and can sometimes be the main driver of a trade idea. I also believe they are overrated as indicators. By many Traders, we discussed sentiment a fair bit in the section on hurting bias, but there are some other important points to cover  Here are the key points on sentiment and positioning.  1 sentiment and positioning matters, sometimes, but not most of the time 2 80 to 90% of the time sentiment just follows price. So if you trade against sentiment and positioning, you're often just fighting the trend and 3, add inflection points attuning feel for positioning and sentiment can be a huge source of edge.  Positioning is packed with potential energy. Like fuel for a future fire.  Positioning unwinds are like fires. They need heat a spark or Catalyst fuel large positions, and oxygen more, bad news or mark-to-market losses, that trigger feedback loops that lead to more position on lines.  You need large positions, a catalyst and mark-to-market losses all at once. Then you get a chain reaction that continues until it finally Burns itself out or it's put out by a central bank.  When I was in grade school, they taught us this process with the fire triangle.  Large positions are like piles of dry Tinder that can potentially burst into flame at any moment but probably won't remember just because you have a large stack of dry wood next to an open can of gasoline, that does not mean there will be a fire. Here's a simple positioning and sentiment framework I use.  Sentiment is bullish and increasing, that's bullish.  Sentiment is bullish and stable still bullish.  Sentiment is bullish. But Calling, that's bearish.  And if sentiment is bearish and stable or bearish in getting more bearish, that's bearish, but if its bearish but getting less bearish that's bullish.  So, essentially what you want to know is the rate of change in sentiment versus the trend, or in other words going against sentiment works at the turning points, but not during the trend, if you can identify a palpable turn in sentiment by observation of behavior, or by a data analysis, you can position ahead of the crowd.  Sources of sentiment and positioning data.  The best source of sentiment at positioning information is knowledgeable, observation of how people discuss a market. This is easier. If you've been trading for a few years and talked to many people in the market, but you can also get a sense of this from reading news and social media. Just remember that many channels are biased.  In terms of data the most commonly used positioning data, comes from the cftc.  This data shows the positioning of non-commercial speculation. While it's closely followed and can be interesting information. It has no predictive power on its own. If you see copper Longs on cftc, hit all-time high. Your instinct is probably to go short copper but the Inconvenient Truth is that a setup like that is actually modestly. Bullish on all-time Horizons.  The cftc data is slow moving and Trend following in its directional. Not contrarian, do not use cftc data by itself as a contrarian indicator.  Options markets, often contain important positioning information. For example, when stocks rise and vix Rises at the same time, you know, that something unusual is going on because the correlation is almost, always inverted between these 2, stocks up. Picks up, is a sign that the market is so bullish. It's adding to upside with calls of hedging. The downside with puts, you can also look at the put call ratio for a read on Equity sentiment, in currency markets. People, look at the risk reversal to see if trade, ERS, have a strong preference for puts or calls and how that preference is changing.  As with most sentiment data, the risk reversal mostly follows price. But when there's a Divergence that is dull and going up, even as the risk reversal is morbid for dollar, puts that can be a signal. That something is a miss.  Positioning surveys can be a useful source of information, their timely and fast. Moving people change their minds quickly as opposed to Trend following systems, which move much more slowly as they wait for Price confirmation. Again with surveys, most of the time, they are directional. The only time they're contrarian is when they hit an extreme in the positioning, surveys, I've done. When working at Banks, I found that when sentiment made a record high or low, for the past year, that could often be a good sign of a turn.  In the stock market. There are many, sentiment, surveys and indicators available. I like the aii survey Bulls versus Bears, CNN's, fear, and greed indicator is also a Simple, snapshot of equity sentiment.  Many banks publish sentiment and positioning data. In FX, there's the city pain index and the HSBC. Positioning indicators in Broad, macro the Bank of America Global fund manager survey gets a lot of attention and highlights crowded positions and tail risks.  Retail Brokers, like Goa dukascopy and daily FX. Publish detailed positioning data.  Each market has its important sentiment, and positioning data. And this is generally easy to find simply by Google as you become expert. In your Market, you'll learn the best sentiment and positioning indicators to follow.  Finally magazine covers can be the ultimate contrarian indicator when a financial team hits a cover of a mainstream magazine. Always take note by the time journalists believe the theme is worthy of a cover story that theme is by definition. Well known. That means the theme is probably also crowded for more on the magazine cover indicator Google. Donnelly magazine cover indicator.  How do you use sentiment and positioning data?  Sentiment and positioning data can help you significantly at turning points. The number 1, Takeaway on sentiment, and positioning whether you're using surveys, options, markets, or other sources of information is that only the super mega extremes are times when you should consider fading.  Otherwise positioning and sentiment are mostly just Trend indicators and they move up and down with the price.  In chapter 9, we discussed has sentiment and positioning of all of, as a market moves through its narrative cycle, I want to mention here that when sentiment is at an extreme and the narrative starts to turn, this is a situation where good sentiment and positioning analysis can make you money.  Extreme Bears sentiment usually reflects that the price has been going down and there's a strong and compelling Bearer story that explains the selloff  Interesting moment comes when the narrative starts to turn good. News starts to emerge but price does not turn higher right away. Confirmation bias, hampers the Judgment of the individual and it also impacts the Judgment of the crowd in aggregate. You'll often see the news flow turn against a major Trend, but no reaction in the price at first  Often Traders with lower confidence will defer to the market. They might say, oh, I guess the news isn't that bullish after all, since the market keeps going lower, while expert Traders, with the strong understanding of the narrative and a positioning, will see an opportunity in the mismatch between price and narrative. They'll say something more like the news flow has clearly turned bullish here but the markets clinging to the old bearish narrative short, positioning is in the 97th percentile and I bet there's going to be a huge reversal soon.  Then that expert Trader will go on high alert for signals. That price has bottomed. This could be as simple as waiting for a moving average to break or waiting for a bullish, reversal pattern like a hammer, ending diagonal, or keyword. personal day, The Savvy Trader sees that the sell-off is set to reverse but Waits  she looks for evidence that a reversal in price is happening and finds setups that allow for a reasonable stop-loss. She knows better than to Simply buy over and over as the price continues to plummet, try not to catch falling knives. Remember what they say about bottom Pickers?  Wait, for an indication that things have turned before you trade against a powerful trend.  In chapter 12, I'll go through a specific example of how I used a Divergence between sentiment and narrative to get set on a counter Trend trade.  Alpha Traders, use multiple approaches to find high. Confidence trades. They wait for multiple indicators to line up.  They use fundamentals narrative analysis, positioning and sentiment, and other factors to get a reading on the market. Then they use technical analysis to fine-tune timing entry and exit. They structure trades in a way that acknowledges that many ideas will be Wrong. So that when they're inevitably wrong, the cost is small.  When they are right, the payoff is large.  Excellent Traders are confident in their views but also know they are frequently wrong.  Some hedge funds, make all their trading decisions using macro analysis but then simply filter every idea with 1 or 2, simple. Technical indicators trade ideas, remain in the off, State until the technical indicator, puts them in the on position.  for example, let's say a fund has a pod comprised of 3, genius analysts and 2, senior portfolio, managers  They currently hold 5 Core views, foolish, dollar Yen, polish Apple, Polish gold, bearish, bonds, and bearish Amazon.  The fund will then systematically apply a simple filter to the ideas. Let's say a 55-day moving average, the Pod will only put on the trades that are on the correct side of the 55-day, moving average, for example, they'll only go short Amazon, if it's below the 55-day, this avoids. Extended periods of wrongness because if Amazon enters a prolonged uptrend, the fund will stay flat the whole time as the stock trends higher and remains above its 55-day. Moving average, when price breaks below the 55-day, the fund will put on their Amazon short. If Amazon closes back above the 55-day, they're out.  This approach of trading macro with a technical filter is an excellent model. It allows you to find your Edge on the analysis side but then forces you to admit you're wrong when price goes against you.  Remember that no approach is perfect and shall be markets. The trend signal will generate a ton of noise and that can be costly. But overall the multifactor fundamental approach with a technical filter is a strong logical approach to Trading. You feel lucky punk?  Data collection risk management and variance.  Most of what I've learned from gambling is also true for investing.  People mostly don't understand risk reward and uncertainty. Their investment results would be better if they did and quote  Edward Thorp a man for all markets.  in this chapter, we'll Discuss risk management. If you are new to trading this chapter will help you build and Implement a robust risk management framework. If you're an experienced professional, I hope you will extract a few nuggets of wisdom and alternative viewpoints and use these observations to make incremental improvements to your current framework.  Risk management is the most important skill in trading. Everything else is a distant. Second, you can have the best ideas, the greatest algorithms, the best instinctive pattern recognition, and the fastest execution systems, but if you're risk management system is not adequate, you will fail.  I could write an entire book about risk management but a that would be boring to write. So I'm not going to do that. And B, the real Essentials of risk management are fairly simple and can be boiled down into 1 chapter.  The big challenges in Risk Management, come from Trading businesses, that employ complex strategies, huge portfolios correlated assets ill, liquid markets, and nonlinear Derivative products.  For short-term directional traders in liquid markets. It's pretty easy to establish. An appropriate set of risk management. Rules the hard part is following them.  Collect and analyze p&l data.  Before you develop a risk management system, you need to build a solid data collection system collecting and analyzing p&l data is 1 of the most important and Powerful activities in trading.  Much in the way. Trading journal provides qualitative feedback. P&l analysis provides quantitative feedback. This does not need to be complicated if you don't already have 1. Create a spreadsheet to track your daily p&l columns, should include date stop-loss, open high, low close,  Date is today's date. Stop-loss is your daily stop-loss today. If you have 1 open is how much you made overnight, that is your p&l. When you start trading in the morning,  This is useful to track. Because it's a different type of trading than what you do during the day.  High and low. Give you a sense of your daily p&l, volatility over time and also what the extremes tend to be.  After collecting a year or so of data, you might notice that you tend to top out at the same level at the same time and then closed lower. If you always peek around the same high, then closed lower consider setting a take-profit target for the day.  Many Traders will consistently have a tough time breaking through a specific level of p&l in a given day?  This tendency to Peak at a specific. P&l level is not random. It reflects a combination of your position size and the volatility of your Market.  if you trade hundred billion dollar positions in a product that tends to move, no more than 1% per day, you're going to notice your p&l tops at around 1 million quite often  To break to a higher Plateau. You first need to know the level of your current Plateau. Then you need to calculate how you can move to a higher level.  The next step to building a quality. Tnl. Tracking spreadsheet is to set up individual pnl columns for each major product and sub product. You trade FX Bond stocks. If your Macro, for example, or individual stocks, if you're an equity day trader that trades particular names  This is highly individual and depends on what you think is worth tracking.  A note of caution here, keep it high level. If you trade 50 stocks, there's not much Point maintaining a column for each stock. There's a point at which your spreadsheet, has so much data that it yields mostly noise and little actionable information.  Note that if you're a day trader, you can't be an expert in 50 different stocks and you shouldn't be trading that many anyway.  Another idea is to set up columns for each strategy, you run. If you have 1 strategy to trade the market open, another 1, that trades during the day and a third strategy that trades on news, you can create 1 column for each strategy to track how they're doing over time.  The idea here is to have data that will help you. Answer, what is working and what is not and then you can do more of what's working and less of what is not.  The spreadsheet should not be more than 12 to 15 columns wide, including month to date and year to date columns on the far right.  If you have more than 15 columns aggregate or combine, some of them, until you can see the whole thing on 1 screen.  There's a point where too much information is bad, be thoughtful about what data might be useful, and collect the data, you can always add new columns later, keep it fairly basic for now.  Once you have about 6 months of data calculate the following, when percent, which is number of winning days, divided by total, number of trading days,  Average winning day in dollars or basis points, average losing day in dollars or basis points.  Win lose dollars or basis points ratio.  This is simply your average winning day, divided by your average losing day.  This is the most important statistic for most short-term Traders. Your piano is simply number of winning days times. Average winning day minus number of losing days times average losing day.  There are just 2 numbers. You need to control your win percent and the ratio of the size of your winning days to the size of your losing days. That's it.  There's no right answer for how these 2 stats should combine but win percent is much more difficult to influence than the relationship between your winning and losing days. Your win percent is usually determined more by your trading style and the seat. You're in, whereas the ratio of your winning Day, pnl to your losing day. Pnl is influenced by your discipline, patience, and ability to cut losses and ride winners.  That ratio tends to be the best reflection of trading skill in my opinion.  I've been keeping these trading stats since 2006 and it's surprising how consistently my win percent. Hovers around 50%.  All my success or failure is determined by the size of my winning days, versus the size of my losing days. And more precisely if I can keep the losers under control, the winners tend to take care of themselves.  I have no trouble making money, but I often have trouble keeping it. This is the profile of the active, risk-seeking Trader?  On the other hand, a skilled. But more risk averse Trader might have a higher win percent. Say 60%, the idea here is to collect enough data. And then start to think about changes, you can make to either increase your win percent or increase the size of your updates, relative to the size of your down days.  Like I said earlier, for most Traders, the up versus down day ratio is easier to influence than the win percent.  What stocks or currencies or Commodities? Do you make money in?  Also, which ones cost you money. Important note here don't overestimate the importance of this information. Good products, and good markets, come and go. I've heard people say, stuff like I keep losing money in euro dollar. It's just stuck in the stupid range. I really need to take it off my screen. In that scenario, the problem is not euro dollar. The problem is that it's in a range and it sounds like the trader isn't very good at trading ranges. If she takes euro dollar offer screen and then it Treads higher for 10, straight days. She's probably going to miss a huge opportunity.  Collect data on specific assets and asset classes, not so you can put them in the Penalty Box, but so you can isolate. What is working and what isn't? And why?  How many times do you breach your stop-loss? What were the causes on each of those days? Anytime you're low? Or close is lower than your stop loss? That's a red flag. Something is wrong with your process. Your positions are too big. You're too slow to stop out. You're out of control in some way if you're a manager, take stop-loss as seriously. Don't increase the trader. Stop when it hits shutter down.  Performance by day of the week. And on event days, are you making all your money on fed days and month end and losing money? Every Monday. Look for patterns that make sense. But be careful not to be Fooled by Randomness here. If you can explain why a pattern exists, it's much less likely that it's an artifact of randomness.  What time of day do you make and lose money on average? This is a bit more work and involves a separate spreadsheet where you take a snapshot of your p&l, every 30 minutes.  After a few months, you'll have an interesting p&l curve, that will show you how your profitability evolves throughout the day.  Are you making all your money on the open then leaking half of it back out over the course of the day, do you lose money consistently from 11:00 a.m. to 3:00 p.m. every day?  Many Traders make all their money in the morning. Then spend the rest of the day. Trying not to give a back. This is dumb. If you make all your money in the morning, shut down at noon and find other ways to be productive. Do research, read, workout, Etc. Even if you work at a bank, nobody's going to force you to trade. In the afternoon. Try to get a strong sense of how time of day influences, your performance. Personally, I make most of my My money overnight and from 7 a.m. to 11:00 a.m. and the period from 11:00 a.m. to 5:00. P.m. New York is a p&l graveyard.  Identify the juicy times to train and act with focused aggression during these times for the lackluster parts of your training day, find replacement activities to keep you occupied. So you do not press buttons at a boredom.  You should also look at monthly and yearly data as time goes on. Ideally, your p&l should skew to the right on every time frame. In other words, your biggest day should be updates. Not down days. Your biggest months should be positive. Not negative, you should rarely lose money in a year and never 2 years in a row. The more Edge you have, the more this paragraph will be true of your trading statistics.  Develop a risk management system.  You have your data collection system. Now, you need a risk management system, this section is about risk management for short-term traders who trade, liquid products and take mostly directional risk. If you sell low, Delta options or run, a 78 line portfolio or buy and sell Frontier Market bonds, this section does not apply to you.  I think the most useful way to explain my risk management system. Thought process is to start with longer time Horizons. Then gradually zoom in to smaller and smaller increments.  I'll start with 1 trading year. Then discuss monthly risk management. Then move all the way down to daily, risk management, and individual trades.  There is no single correct answer when it comes to managing risk, your risk management system has to match your personality, your time Horizon, your trading style, your Capital, your access to leverage and the liquidity profile of the products you trade. I'm going to outline how I think about risk management and give you some of the details of my risk management system. Take what makes sense to you, discard what does not  Yearly risk management.  In most trading jobs 1, year is the most important unit of trading performance. Almost all bank and Real Money traders are paid based on annual performance and most hedge funds. Also use the calendar year as their main unit of time for evaluation and compensation.  Sure, some hedge funds pay out more than once per year and Retail Traders, don't think as much about yearly increments, but all Traders should think about monitor analyze and Target. Yearly performance.  In order to survive in the long run, you want your yearly performance to look like that of a call option?  This means flat or perhaps small losses in the backyards and medium-sized to huge games in the good years.  20 years of performance for a skilled adoptable Trader using the risk management style of described in this chapter might look something like the chart in the PDF.  that chart shows 20 years of trading for a skilled Trader who behaves like a call option,  To achieve this sort of distribution of p&l, you need to do 3 things.  1 start, slow and increase risk, as you Here today. P&l builds.  You cannot have the same risk appetite on January 1 with zero P and L on the coffers as you can on August 15th.  This means there must be an arbitrary change in your risk appetite each January but that's just the way things go.  In an ideal world, you should always be varying your risk appetite based on conviction and Market opportunity but we live in the real world as such you need to start slow and build risk over time.  There's something special and scary about the zero mound in pno. Let's say a hypothetical Trader named Steven Kirk wants to make 10 million this year and a stop-loss is $4 million.  You will find that going from plus million dollars to plus 1 million feels significantly less bad than going from plus 500 K to minus 500 K, even though both are pnl declines of dollars.  Any red number next to your name? Feels bad.  This is consistent with Theory, which tells us that if a gain gives us X pleasure, a loss of the same magnitude gives us 2x pain.  This is not all just psychological though.  As Steve's pnl Falls below zero. His ability to take risk. That is his leverage decreases. There's a nonlinear real world impact of losses. As he lives below zero and closer to his stop loss. When you're below 0.0, you wear a straight jacket.  Let's say it's February 15th has Steve psychology at year to date piano of 750,000. He's not ecstatic because he's a bit behind plan but not too bad. How about it year to date? P&l is zero still, okay? Probably at minus 1 million, he's getting a bit nervous that minus 2 million. Steve is halfway to a stop loss. After only 6 weeks,  At that point, he's going to need to reduce his risk in order to avoid hitting the disaster Zone, which is around 3 million. Remember, even if your stop loss is in big trouble trading wise much before then because you'll need to reduce your position size in order to avoid getting the shoulder tap.  You need to start the year slow and build up.  The year has a great deal of path dependence which means that how you start has a big influence on where you end up if Steve loses 2 million dollars in January the odds that he will hit is 10 million dollar goal are almost zero. If he makes 400 K in January and 600 K in February, he's behind budget but still in an okay position.  The idea is to get safely away from the zero bound and then when a mega opportunity arrives and you have a cushion, go for it.  If Steve's plus 1.5 million on February 25th and there's a fantastic opportunity. He's in a position to risk, a bigger chunk of cash, say 500 K on the idea without worrying about putting himself underwater  Remember that rule number 1 of trading is don't blow up. Always give yourself a chance to play tomorrow.  It's tricky to give precise mathematical formulas for risk management because this implies there is a correct answer. There is no precise answer in Risk Management.  Every Trader, every bank, and every hedge fund must manage risk differently. What I can tell you is how I think about risk in different scenarios. You can either mirror parts of this framework. If it makes sense to you or you can transform it to fit your needs.  Before I can establish my basic yearly framework. I need to know my free capital.  Free capital is the amount of money you can afford to lose for retail Traders. That's the portion of your account, you feel, you can lose in a year and still come back.  For Bank, real money and hedge fund Traders. It's your annual stop loss.  My strategy is to start slow and build up Capital then get as aggressive as possible. If there are juicy Market opportunities by June or July at the latest hopefully earlier.  Budgets vary over the years but I will again. Is the 104 million dollar stop loss because that's a realistic ratio and the absolute numbers don't matter. As much as the relative figures.  Using the same 2.5 to 1 ratio, a retail Trader, might be risking a maximum loss of dollars, for the year, with the goal of making a 100,000, a hedge fund Trader, might have a stop loss of 40 million and a target of 100 million.  This 2.5 to 1 ratio is not brutal. It's just a realistic ratio that's in the ballpark.  Some Bank Traders might have a tighter stop loss of 2 million and be looking to make 20 million. If they have a great market-making seat, a retail Trader might be risking 20,000 but hope to make 200,000  Again, the numbers are presenting in this chapter are not meant to be immutable laws of Finance. They are realistic parameters that work in my world but may need to be adjusted in yours.  A Trader with a maximum loss of 4 million and a target of 10 million can allow more risk-taking when he's doing. It should take less risk when he's struggling based on year to today. Pnl, a good system is to allocate a new monthly stop on the first day of the month and determine that stop-loss using the year to date pnl. The better, you're doing the more leverage you use in the next month. If good opportunities arise  In the PDF, I show a table that shows a reasonable scale for the $7 stop-loss Trader. Who wants to make $1. The objective is never to get to your year-to-date goal and then just put your feet up. The objective is to get stronger and stronger and stronger over the course of the year. So you can take maximum risk when rare opportunities arise under the sort of framework. You might find yourself up 6 million in June and able to spend 1 million on a strong trade idea, that makes 4 million  Then you're up 10 million in July. You can sit on your hands until the next huge opportunity comes.  If your goal for the year was 10 million and you're there in July, you put yourself in a position to make 20 million. Or maybe more those chances. Don't come along every year. Keep Trading.  Often the p&l chart of an alpha macro Trader in a good year. Will look like a series of steps with interval periods where the p&l flat lines. This is the p&l trajectory of a Trader that waits for the big opportunities and then puts on meaningful risk at the right times. This is not the trajectory for every Alpha Trader, a good retail, scalper or news Trader, for example, will have a p&l chart that looks more like a 45 degree angle,  but even if a Trader that's consistent, should be taking more risk and making more money when they're strongly positive for the year and taking less risk, when they are close to zero or negative year to date.  2, understand the metagame.  Trading successfully and moving the risk level up and down throughout the year. Requires a good understanding of the specific metagame. You are playing  You need to understand the internal rules incentives, politics and risk management framework, where you work and incorporate. Ate these into your risk management decisions as well.  For example, does your boss freak out when you lose more money than x? If so you know that that is the internal no-go line. Even if there's never been a rule set, does your contract say you'll get fired if you lose more than 9%? Well, history shows that traders that your firm tend to get de-risked or fired when they lose 5%.  Forget the number in the contract. The metagame rules say your stop loss should be 5%.  If you work at a bank, does your compensation Flatline? Once your p&l hits a certain threshold?  If you yes then keep in mind. If you hit that threshold losses will cost you more in compensation than gains will help you adjust your risk accordingly.  There are so many different meta games depending on where you work. Take a few minutes to think about the metagame in your seat. Even if you're a retail Trader, there's probably a metag game. For example, if you don't earn a certain amount of money in your first 2 years, will your spouse get antsy and ask you to get a real job. If so then you need to set your target above that level.  You need your family on board or you will find life difficult. Trading is incredibly hard. It will. Inevitably test your belief in yourself. If those around you management family, the person who provided your trading Capital, don't believe in you, it will be impossible.  3, don't blow up.  This 1 so simple. And yet Traders, blow up all the time, trade liquid products. If you're a retail Equity Trader, don't short the biotech, that just tripled from 1 150 to 450. If you're a currency Trader, don't take positions in pegged, currencies.  Manage your overnight and weekend risk tightly.  the smaller, your account size are free Capital, the more careful you need to be,  If you sell options, think about ridiculous worst case scenarios and avoid them.  The universe of Trades is so large in every asset class. There's no excuse for putting on trades that have unlimited downside. You should be confident about your ability to stop out of any trade. Or you should keep it small enough, it's not going to kill you if it goes pear-shaped.  And has a low probability of a huge tail risk, cover it and find something better to trade.  Path. Dependence is an important concept for every professional Trader to understand it comes into play in different ways path. Dependence means future steps of a process depend on its history. A series of coin flips is not path. Dependent.  If you flip 10 tales the coin has no memory. And the 11th flip is fair. And 50/50, on the other hand each month in a trading year is path, dependent your performance. In January will determine your free capital in your state of mind for February.  The most important thing to remember is to never take actions in 1, period, that could be overly detrimental to Future periods.  For example, you work at a bank where p&l resets every January 1st, there's a perfect 5-star setup. In S&P futures And you've never been so confident there about to finally crash your approach to this trade, the sizing, you choose and the amount of capital you'll risk should be different in January than in August.  Well this is irrational from a theoretical point of view in real life. There's too much path dependence in January for you to go all in. You must have conservatively knowing that your performance in January. Will influence your performance in the following 11 months.  On the other hand, if your annual Target was 5 million, and you're now up 7 million and it's September, you know, that even if you lose 2 million on this trade idea, it won't have much negative impact on your trading for the final 3 months of the year.  I know this might not seem rational to you but believe me it is playing the multi period, metagame correctly. Is just as important as maximizing, p&l for a given period,  Another way path. Dependence matters is when you start a new job in training, a Trader that is made money 8. Straight years, doesn't attract much attention. If she loses money in year 9 on the other hand, a new hire that loses money in his first 4 months of training, might make a manager question. Why they hired him.  Financial firms. Are generally not very good at separating luck from skill or at comparing Market regimes to Trader performance.  The absolute number next to your name matters much more than any context surrounding it.  Therefore when you start a new trading job, it is crucial that you understand how much runway you have.  As ridiculous as it sounds, your ultimate success or failure. At a hedge fund is usually determined within the first 18 months.  Your entire career or lack of career at the fund will be rooted in that initial performance, the trader that comes out hot and shoots, the lights out will be given, plenty of slack going forward and could probably draw down in future without too much hassle.  The equally skilled Trader that comes in and loses money for the first 5 months will be under heightened scrutiny. Even if those 5 Months where the lowest volatility period in the history of his asset class, and he's known to be a Trader that performs well in high ball markets.  The way to protect against this path. Dependence, when switching firms is to ensure, you have at least 1 and hopefully, 2 or 3 strong supporters within the organization.  And make sure that the strong supporter who brought you in is planning to stay.  Once your sponsor leaves your life or death in that firm becomes even more path dependent. Try to avoid relying on luck by ensuring that you have sufficient Runway. When you go to a new firm,  Jerry, the greatest Trader Who Never Was. At the start of each month, you should take stock of your year-to-date performance and decide on a reasonable goal and stop loss for the month ahead.  As the year progresses you have more ammunition to take more risk. So if you've been doing well, increase your monthly allocation, if you're struggling, keep it flat or cut it.  Keep in mind that increasing your risk, allocation does not mean you have to start taking bigger positions on the first day of the month, what it means is that when a juicy opportunity arrives in the new month, you allocate more to it than you would have in the prior month. Just, before the start of a new month, you should determine monthly stop-loss monthly profit goal.  Dollars at risk for type 1. Type 2 and type 3 trades which I'll Define soon and 1 personal goal of Fitness, sleep eating Etc.  As we move down the fractals from yearly, to monthly to daily to single trades, there's an important concept you should have in your mind variance.  Let's talk about variance for a few minutes because it's a crucial Concept in trading.  First, here's a quote that says it up nicely.  Quote, the laws of probability so true in general. So fallacious in particular and quote, Edward Gibbon 17774.  In statistics and probability variance, is the concept that in a series of numbers with a given average, individual data points will be spread out away from the average. So even if your average daily p&l over the last 10 years is plus 23,000, you might still lose 25,000 5 days in a row once. This may not mean you did anything wrong, it's just variance.  Variance is a prominent feature in poker a well-rested? Professional. Poker player will inevitably beat a drunk amateur over a long period. Say 12 hours, but over a 10-minute or even 30 minute period, the drunk amateur could easily prevent  The key to understanding variance is that it's most visible in small samples, the larger the sample, the closer, the aggregate data will approach. The true. Average luck is a short-term thing, but in the long run skill prevails  Then again, as keen's famously said in the long run, we're all dead.  A very solid trading strategy with significant Edge generate substantial variance even year to year. This is why it's incredibly difficult to evaluate Traders portfolio managers and hedge funds. Was it luck or was it skill?  Michael mobus and his book, The Luck equation, untangling skill and luck in business is the go-to book. On this topic.  To give you a sense of how variants impacts a year of performance and training. I ran a few simulations for Simplicity. We'll use a static risk management approach where the trader does not change their risk appetite during the year regardless of performance.  That said, I strongly Advocate increasing your risk as your pnl Rises.  I include these simulations because I think very few people even expert trainers. Truly appreciate just how dramatic the impact of variance can be on performance.  Probably in defense mechanism.  If we truly appreciate it, how important variants was, we'd feel like we barely control our own fate and humans, love to believe they're in control.  The simulations are in the PDF. The first 1 approximates, the performance of a retail Trader, she makes money 50% of days and loses money. 50% of days, her average winning day is $2,000. Net and her average losing day is -200 based on these figures. She'll Expect to make 100,800 in an average year.  But when we simulate her cumulative year to date, p&l 100 times, it looks much noisier than that, of course.  The same Trader using the same strategy in a world. With the same probabilities might make 50,000 1 year and 150,000 the next  With variance and literally nothing else to blame.  The second simulation represents a bag Trader. He makes money 55% of days and loses money. 45% of days, his average winning day. Net is 150,000 and his average losing day is minus 120,000.  Based on these figures, his expected value is plus 7.2 million, but take a look at the 100ul in the PDF.  You can see that despite employing a process with substantial Edge and positive expected value his annual performance is all over the place, simply due to variance.  This is a big reason why it is so FL. Something difficult for management to properly, assess Traders, and for Traders to evaluate themselves, it's easy to be Fooled by Randomness.  1 of the most difficult and most important skills in trading is the ability to distinguish between variants and bad decision-making, if you're losing money right now. Is it because your process has broken down. Your discipline sucks and the market is completely changed.  Or is it because of the inevitable variance within the p&l distribution.  When you're on a bad streak, you need to ask yourself this.  Longer you've been trading the easier. This is to answer for me, I can usually look back on a losing streak and determine what the fair amount of confidence. Whether it was variance when you're starting out, it's almost impossible to tell  Here are some examples of how I look at a losing streak and determine whether it's a product of variance or bad process.  Variance 1. I was wrong. A lot being wrong. Is part of trading. It's okay to be wrong 2. I got unlucky. I was long. And some bad news came out yesterday today. I got stopped out at the highs.  When I'm wrong and I lose money. I barely care that's trading. As long as I've been sticking to my risk management system. I shrug and move on being wrong, is where variants comes from in trading. Don't worry about it.  On the other hand, some things can't be attributed to variants, not variants 1. I've been right? And I'm reading the market, well, but I'm losing money. This is a strong indicator that there's something wrong with my process or risk management for much of 2020. I was in sync with the market and my percentage of accurate calls was very high, but my performance was disappointing because of multiple breakdowns and discipline and process.  I was wrong sizing, my trades, and bleeding on random low conviction ideas, if you're reading the market, well, and losing money, that's a red flag. Something is wrong with your process and it's not just variance.  2, I traded with no plan, I walked in each day and started hitting by. Buttons without a good sense of what I was trying to do. I cut out a good ideas early because I was bored.  3, I'm overtrading.  I should be tight aggressive, but instead, I'm making a series of small bets on random dumb ideas and leaking money every day.  4. I left a stale order in the system overnight and it triggered a loss. Poor attention to detail is not bad luck.  5, my discipline is poor. I broke my risk management. Rules, my positions were way too big. I kept moving my stop lowering of cutting. I doubled a losing position.  6. The market regime is changed and I have not adapted 7. My boss is giving me a hard time and making me nervous 8 if I keep losing money, I'm going to lose my job.  9, I'm tired. I'm arguing with my spouse or children. I think I might get a divorce. I'm drinking too much. I feel depressed. I don't want to do this anymore.  If loss is stem from any of 1 to 9 above, that's not very ants. That's bad trading, bad process or bad mindset cut all your risk and figure out how to get things back on track.  when you trust yourself to do the right thing, you're trading well,  It should not matter all that much whether you make or lose money on any given day.  When you're trading with a good mindset, you see each trade and each day as a series of independent events with a probabilistic nature and you understand that some trades and some days, you will lose money.  Each day you come in and do your best without getting too high or too low.  You invest most of your energy in the process in very little in the outcome. Each trade you go through a thoughtful process and execute in a rational and systematic way.  If you trade sloppy, don't say I suck. Or I traded so bad today instead. Ask, why was I so sloppy today? Am I burnt out? Discouraged disengaged robotic application of a process? Keeps you sane? And lets, you know, you can trust yourself if you break your stop-loss repeatedly. You should quickly figure out why that discipline no automation of stop-loss. Don't care anymore.  If you go through the above lists variants versus not variants and are still not sure whether a recent losing streak is caused by variants or bad trading. Take a step back.  The more experience you have the easier it is to make this determination. But if you're new, it can be very difficult especially if you tend to be critical of yourself.  Traders with the healthy mindset who are confident in their process. Have a much easier time dealing with losing streaks because they can confidently identify the cause as variance and move on.  Traders with the less healthy mindset or less confidence will tend to assume that a losing streak is the result of bad decisions and a lack of trading skill.  Alpha Traders with the solid process. And excellent risk management. Still hit losing streaks in the long run. A solid process will win. But in the short run, there's always going to be ups and downs due to variance. If you make money 50% a day, you Can expect to lose money 6 or 7 days in a row once in a while that's fine.  When you lose money, 6 days in a row and determine that it's simply because of variance. You need to double down on your process and believe in what you're doing more than ever variance, can be managed, but never controlled get comfortable with variance.  If you understand your Edge and can confidently, explain it, you'll be much more comfortable with variance. Furthermore, if you continually spend time thinking about the current regime and how to adapt to present market conditions, you will reduce your variance and maintain steadier performance over time.  No matter how good you are though, you'll still need to understand that variance is a thing.  Daily risk management.  Daily. Stop losses can be good for some styles of trading and counterproductive for others.  Daily stops work, best for very short-term day Traders. If your time Horizons, generally, 2 minutes, to 2 hours, Any square up your risk at the end of each day a daily stop-loss is probably right for you if you trade a 1-week Time. Horizon and often by short dated options, a daily stop-loss makes no sense.  For retail Traders, 1 of the simplest Risk Management Systems is to Simply multiply your free Capital by 1% every morning and risk that much and no more, then start fresh and do the same thing. Tomorrow, this increases your risk. As you make money, decreases it as you lose money and keeps you in the game. If you're new to trading and plan to trade short-term views, only I highly recommend this approach. It is extremely conservative, but not ridiculously conservative. It will keep you safe, but give you plenty of opportunity to succeed.  Daily stop losses are best for retail Traders, day Traders and Junior institutional Traders, but less useful for most institutional or macro Traders.  Daily stop-losses. Create a timing mismatch for Traders, whose time Horizon span. be on 1 day, they make it difficult to ride, impulsive multi-day moves,  Say you're a bank Trader. With a 250,000 stop-loss, a $1 million monthly, stop-loss and a 10 million dollar budget.  Some huge news comes out and it looks like a game-changer for Euro. You buy 50 million euros at 1:18 with a 50-point stop-loss risking 250,000 euro dollar, ribs, 50 Pips higher, 15 minutes and ends the day 100 Pips higher at 1119 0, you're up 500,000 on the day and think the move will continue.  You believe the logical stop is your original entry, minus 10 Pips, which is 11790 because you're a big fan of news, pivots and feel. If you're a dollar goes back below the news pivot, which was 1/18, where you bought, the news has been rejected,  If you go home long, you're a dollar with the stuff at 11790. Your total risk on the trade is just 50,000.  but if you get stopped out your daily, You know, tomorrow will be the 500,000 you made today. Plus the $1,000 loss which is 550,000.  That's way more than your daily stop of 250,000. So you either need to cut your risk. By more than half or you need to move your stop closer. This is the tricky part of marketing to Market each day,  Very often in a case like this, you'll see the trader. Simply put their stop 50 points or less away in order to stay below their daily limit. Instead of trading the market, they're trading their p&l inevitably. There's a 60-point dip overnight. The trader gets stopped out before Euro goes on a 400. 400 Point topside Rampage  On the other hand, if the trader limits match their time Horizon, there'll be no such problem. If this Trader is running with the 1 million dollar, monthly stop, they can trade the market. The way they believe it is meant to be traded. Knowing that, if all goes horribly wrong, all they lost was 50,000. They can get back on their horse and keep riding the next day.  The key with any. Risk management system is that it needs to match your free Capital, your trading style and your time Horizon.  Daily stops don't work with week-long ideas, monthly stops. Don't make much sense for day Traders other than as a backup or overlay to a daily stop-loss system.  Individual trade risk management.  Managing the risk of an individual trade starts with understanding what kind of trade it is. Is this a standard day trade, the type of trade you do almost every day or is this a super rare opportunity that comes around a few times per year?  Clearly, those 2 scenarios require a different position size and risk management approach.  Before I go through exactly how I choose position sizes. Let's talk about conviction first.  Conviction level is 1 of the critical inputs into sizing your position. Good Traders vary their bet size based on conviction that said. If your conviction on a trade idea is not somewhere between high and very high, you probably should not do the trade. Once you find a trade, you like and determine that your conviction is high, can you really extract a deeper level of granularity and determine what trades are somewhat high high or very high conviction. My experience suggests that most of the time you cannot  You're unlikely to be able to judge xanti which routine, day trades, or short-term swing trades, have a higher probability of success. On the other hand, there are some outlier trades that are clearly different from the norm, and will come with a higher level of conviction.  Most good Traders. Use some sort of tiered risk management system. The most common system involves 3 levels, a b, and c trades or 3 star 4-star. And 5-star trades are common ways of labeling conviction.  These systems are simple and they work but there's a fundamental error in most of these systems the increase in risk. As you move up the scale, is way too linear. I believe, risks should increase exponentially on super high. Conviction trades.  the reason is that almost All trades have similar conviction. When you do a day trade or trade, some lead leg relationship or execute against a pattern, you see at the equity open. Those are the bread and butter trades. You do all day every day,  my view is that attaching a finer granular. Determination of conviction or confidence level on those trades, is kind of pointless.  There is however an important exception once every 3 to 6 months, an unbelievably attractive trade comes along, and you should allocate much more Capital than usual to those trades.  I'm talking about those rare opportunities where the timing risk reward and probability of success, all come together in a highly unusual and attractive way.  The clearest examples of this can happen at a watershed moment for your asset Market.  for example, long hedge, Nick features in 2012 for economics the day, they add your favorite stock to the S&P 500 short, the Bitcoin bubble on the day features started trading in 2017  Short. Chesapeake stock. Where the market build it up even though they were bankrupt, these moments that are nearly once in a lifetime require a different approach there, can also be these super high probability trades that are less epic in terms of news flow, but where all the planets? Align,  For me, there are 3 levels of conviction.  Type 1 run-of-the-mill trade you would do on any given day your convictions high or you do not do the trade. This is not a rare opportunity. It's part of your regular process allocate 1% to 3% of your free Capital depending on your Edge experience, level and historical performance.  If you're new, keep to the 1% area newish, stick to 2% or below. And if you're comfortable with your process and know your success rate and statistics, well enough Venture up to 3%, Max  Type 2 high conviction. This is a trade where something unique is going on, but not a watershed moment or extremely rare event. Maybe the FED has turned hawkish after a period of Doubt or a stalk your expert. Has a meaningful change of narrative. A 3-year Trend in oil, looks ready to reverse. A Saudi Arabia has said, Nomas to production cuts.  Type 2 trades. Almost always involve a big narrative shift or some sort of significant dislocation. These are not routine trades. Allocated meaningful chunk of capital to the idea. Based on your predetermined parameters, 3% to 5% of free Capital generally makes sense, but if you're having an excellent year you may want to add 10% of your year to date profits on top.  The way you get outside upside with limited downside, is you reinvest profits, when excellent opportunities arise a type 2 Opportunity. At the start of the year is a bummer because you cannot invest Beyond 3% or maybe 5% Max of free capital on the other hand, if you're having a great year and it's only June, you can over bet the type 2 Opportunity, knowing if you're wrong, it's completely fine.  Let's say a high. Net worth individual has given you a hundred million dollars. Of capital to trade with instructions to shoot for 15% returns and a $1 million stop loss that is stopped trading. If you drop to 95 million,  On January 1, your free capital is 5 million. Your target is 15 million.  Your compensation is simple. You get paid. 20% of your p&l in cash once per year.  Now, it's June your p&l is + 11 million and a type 2 opportunity presents itself.  Remember free capital is your stop loss plus your year to date pnl. So free capital in the situation is the $5 million. Stop loss plus the 11 million year to date pnl which is 16 million.  Scenario. You're ready for anything, that sounds a bit dovish because that might be a catalyst for your reversal trade, which is short dollars.  229 and 45 seconds. 15 seconds to the FED announcement. You plan to hold this position for a multi-day drop of around 4% in the dollar.  Now, you need to determine how much Capital to risk on the idea. It's not a once-in-a-lifetime trade but it's also clearly not a type 1. How do you decide how much to risk?  As a baseline, start with the simple high-end type 1 or low-end type 2 risk calculation.  That would be around, 3% of free Capital, free capital is 16 million. As I said, 3% of 16 million is 480,000. Okay, so that's a bare minimum but that's high. Type 1 risk, sizing, not type 2.  You get outside returns or right tail by making big bets when you're in a strong Capital position. You're more than 2/3 of the way to your yearly goal and it's only June type 2 opportunities are rare in this situation. A simple formula for type 2 is Type 2 risk. Equals 3% of free Capital plus 10% of year-to-date p&l.  In this case that's 480,000 plus 1.1 million, which is 1.58 million.  I like this formula it's simple, it's effective but you don't have to stick to it. Religiously think about how you'll feel. If you draw down that much is it going to kill you psychologically 11 million minus 1.58 million will take you to around 9 and a half million year to date.  That's still not too shabby and in my opinion that would be perfectly manageable.  This is a good starting point and you can always scale up or down from there. Depending on other factors such as how strongly you feel about the trade and how sensitive your manager is to large draw Downs.  this also depends somewhat on how loose, or Rotate. You are with defining trades as type 1, or type 2.  If you're finding yourself thinking, 2 trades per month or type 2 trades, you need to be more conservative. Managers hate surprises, whether you work at a bank hedge fund or other institution, my experience is that all managers prefer a Trader that overcomes Type 3 outlier opportunity.  This is a dislocation policy change, historic moment or economic event so large it Demands, a huge bet but only if you're in a strong position to make that outsized bet.  If you're down on the year or near your monthly stop-loss or near boss man's shoulder tap level, unfortunately, you just have to keep it small allocate type 1 or type 2 money and hope you're in a stronger position next time. But if you're in a strong Capital position, you should rationally assess. What is the most? You can invest in this idea, keep in mind, no matter how good the opportunity might seem, you could still be wrong, you could still lose money. In fact, some people believe there's a point on the confidence curve where you're more likely to lose money. That is you become overconfident.  No matter how great the opportunity you must remember that a your assessment of the probabilities may not be accurate and B you only get to run the experiment. Once you never bet more than you can afford to lose. Roulette wheel will sometimes come up green and you'll occasionally roll a 1 on a 20-sided die.  The point with these type 3 opportunities is that they can make your year, they can make your career and I think in general good Traders, know them when they see them.  if a Type 3 opportunity presents itself, think long and hard about the optimal bet size have courage go big  Here's a short excerpt from Stand drunken Miller's famous lost tree Club speech. When a Type 3 trade comes along, and you're in a strong position, you need to take the most possible risk, that could mean, as much as 50% of your year to date pnl, depending on your risk management framework. And your role  I cannot tell you an exact formula because it depends too much on where you work and how you manage your risk. But start with the type 2 formula and then increase as much as possible. Without introducing any major path dependence problems.  In other words risk as much as you possibly can knowing you still might be wrong and you don't want to ruin the rest of your year by destroying your momentum? Yes. You want to be a pig in situations like this but you also always need to consider. You could be wrong.  Be like a call option.  I've created a simple graphic in the PDF to show you why it's important to structure your process so that you take more risk when you're doing well. Unless When you are not.  I show a p&l simulation for 3 traders, who start out with $100,000 risk, 3% of capital each day make money. 50% of days and their average pnl on winning days is 1.5 times the average p&l on a losing day.
