The zero Factor Trader risks, 3% each day, the 10% Factor Trader risks. 3% of free Capital plus 10% of the year to date pnl and the 20% Factor Trader risks. 3% of free Capital plus 20% of year to date.  You can see the downside similar for all 3 Traditions, a bad year is going to be around. This is because of the trader struggling, they reduce risk and all 3 methodology  Well, a great year. For the zero Factor Trader is more like dollars more upside with that more downside. Nice.  Now, why not go to 50% or 100% of year to date, then?  In an experiment with unlimited time and infinite trials, higher percentages are best but in real life where you only get to live each year, once lower numbers are preferable.  Higher numbers, introduce unacceptable levels of variance. Even 20% is very high. I'm just using it to make the point and show you how the average annual returns vary. As you tweak, your risk factor higher. This is a toned down version of the Kelly Criterion at work.  The Kelly Criterion is a formula used by gamblers to calculate optimal bet size. Using the Kelly Criterion leads to higher wealth. Compared to any other bet sizing strategy in the long run.  The Kelly Criterion relies on known odds and so it's not directly applicable to trading because the probability of winning or losing in trading, can only be Loosely estimated it. Not directly observable. Still the concept behind the Kelly Criterion is directly applicable to trading and risk management even if the precise mathematics are not.  For an entertaining book that includes plenty of information about the Kelly Criterion check out fortunes formula by William Poundstone. It's a great book.  As you make money, you take more risk as you lose. You, take less. This is the core of how you behave like a call option. Over the years, a hedge fund or trading business with a bunch of Traders, all behaving. This way, will have low downside volatility with a ton of upside  When I suggest you allocate 1% to 3% of your free Capital to type 1 trades, this assumes, you have only 1 trade on at a time. If you have more than 1 trade on, you need to very carefully assess the correlation of the trades and the probability that they could all go wrong at the same time.  That's why fewer trades equals better trading equals lower transaction costs. Equals easier risk management. Generally when I have more than 1 type 1 trade on, at the same time, I just treat them all as 1 trade, for risk management purposes.  In other words, I size my position. So that if every single 1 got stopped out, I will not lose more than 3% of my free capital.  Life is much easier if you trade 1 position at a time or treat. All your positions as 1 risk management blob.  If you try to take on more risk and start assuming correlation between different trades, you might suddenly wake up 1 morning down 8% of free Capital because the correlations didn't work out the way you thought they would.  Furthermore, do you want to be an investor or a Trader?  If you have 14 line items you are portfolio manager or an investor but not really a Trader. Do you have 14 good ideas at 1 time? Probably not do you have 7 good ideas at 1 time again probably not focus on 1 or 2 strong ideas and monetize them. Concentrate your beds in the place. You think you have the most Edge and Highest conviction spreading bets around is a recipe for higher transaction costs. Lazy risk, management, diluted returns and correlation confusion.  The big takeaway from this section is that when an epic Watershed trade comes along and you're in a strong Capital position, it's time to go all in  All in does not mean, you risk your job or risk your fund. It means risk. The maximum possible with the parameters. You have set play tight when you're weak, play, extremely aggressively when you're strong and the opportunity is exceptional.  Huge opportunities do not come along all that often jump on them.  Good trading takes courage.  Position sizing.  1 of the most common questions I get from New Traders, is how do you size your positions for me? The answer is pretty simple. Here are the 5 Steps 1. You have a trade idea. Let's say, you think bonds are going to sell off in the days, leading up to non-farm payrolls. That is the jobs report. Because the market is massively long, everyone's been super bullish for months, and the whisper number for the jobs. Number is creeping higher and higher in recent days.  This is a classic run-up trade as described in the previous chapter.  You've decided the best way to trade. This is to buy TBT the ultra Short Bond Market ETF.  While TBT is not a good way to short the bond market for more than a day or 2. Your time Horizon is just 2 days so you've decided this will be the easiest most liquid and levered way to execute the trade.  2.  Determine your conviction level.  You think this is a classic run-up trade and you love a good run-up trade.  You decide, this is a type 2 trade, which this month means. 250,000 of risk. Would normally be allocated to this idea.  3 decide how much to risk you're up 1 million dollars this year and have a particularly strong feeling about this trade. The chart setup is Was great too. So you can take a huge position.  Since you plan to cover the trade before the non-farm payrolls release, you don't have to worry about Gap risk through the number.  You decide to risk the standard 250,000 plus. 10% of your year to date p&l of $1 million. So you're risking 250,000 plus 100,000, which is 350,000 on this trade?  4, determine the stop loss.  Use technical analysis and other inputs like average, daily range expected, volatility. And an understanding of round number bias to determine the stop loss on the trade.  You have a rule that you always place your stop at least 1, average, day's range away from your entry point. This is a good rule of thumb for new Traders. If I know absolutely nothing about a security. The first thing I do when looking to put in a stop-loss is calculate the Securities average daily range, any stop-loss closer than 1 day's range away is at High Risk of getting dinged by short-term noise.  5 determine the position size to make the math. Easy, let's say TBT is trading at 100 and you've determined after looking at the charts and studying the volatility of tbt that the appropriate stop-loss is 97.98.  Liquidity is excellent and slippage is not an issue. You estimate 3 sets of slippage on your stop. So you're risking 2 dollars a share and you decided in step 3 to risk 350,000  Now, here's how you calculate the correct sizing.  Position size is the dollars. You're going to risk divided by the entry point, minus the stop. So in this case 350,000 divided by 2.05, which is 170,731  Therefore, the trader in this example should buy 170,731 shares of tbt. The formula for determining position size is always the same.  Position size equals dollars at risk divided by entry point. Minus stop-loss.  Let's do 1 more just to make sure it's clear.  I'm a retail Trader. I want to sell Aussie dollar because reasons.  The currency pair is trading at 0.75 0 right now. And after studying the pairs volatility and doing 20 minutes of technical analysis, I've determined that 0.75%.  My trading account balance is currently 165,000. I always risk 2% of capital on every trade. I never run more than 1, trade at a time. So this is a safe way to run my account.  I'll add 3 Pips To My stop-loss Level to allow for slippage on execution.  2% of 165,000 is 3,300, so that's my normal risk unit. So my position size is 33. 00, divided by the entry point. Minus the stop-loss, in this case, my stop-loss is 0.007 or 75 Pips.  When you divide 3,300 by 0.075, you get 440,000. So our short position in Aussie dollar should be 440,000 Aussie. If we get stopped out we'll lose 3,300, which is 2% of capital.  Bad Traders work in the opposite direction. They say, I'm short 2 million, Aussie at 75, but I can't afford to lose more than 10 grand. So my Stop's going to be 75.50.  this makes no sense because 7,550 is a totally arbitrary level that does not take Market factors like volatility or technical analysis into consideration,  Always start with dollars at risk, then determine your stop-loss by analyzing the market. The position size is the output not the input.  To summarize the 5 Steps. I just explained. You have a trade idea.  2, determine your conviction, level 3, decide how much to risk the amount to risk can be in dollars or basis points. Depending where you work, the amount is determined by your conviction level year to date. Pnl and your risk management system 4, determine your stop-loss using technical analysis and other inputs 5. Do the math to back out the correct position size.  Using this methodology your position size can vary. Substantially a low conviction position with a wide stop loss for a good hedge fund trade. Might be 20 million Aussie while a high conviction trade with a tighter, stop for the same Trader. Could be 200, million, Aussie, good Traders, vary. Bet size, good Traders, Barry bet size,  Good Traders. Barry bet size whenever you see a Trader that always trades in a thousand share, Lots or 10 million dollar FX positions, there's a good chance, they don't know what they're doing.  Alternatively, some Traders. Are able to vary their position size to a point, but hit a plateau above, a certain amount of risk, they feel uncomfortable and dial it back, even if that's not the optimal sizing, keep an eye out for plateaus like this in your training.  If you tend to top out at a specific risk level, even when you know, your capital and risk management system, allow you to take more risk, figure out why?  The best Traders have the largest variation in bad size, a great Trader might trade lower conviction, size of a thousand shares of Apple early. In the year then 15,000 shares of Apple. By summer when she has high conviction and a big chunk of p&l to work with  Let me clarify something important here, when I talk about increasing your risk. As your p&l grows, I'm not saying you keep doubling down until you bust. There's a bias known as the house money effect, in which studies show that Traders and gamblers, treat money. They've won differently for money. They started out with. If you start the night at a casino with a 100 bucks and now you have $100. To incremental dollars feels different than the initial 100. It's free money.  Studies show that gamblers treat house money with less respect and are more likely to gamble. It away recklessly don't do that, that's not what I'm recommending.  What I'm talking about is a methodical increase in Risk, appetite as your bank. Roll grows, this is like the Kelly Criterion in sports gambling, as your bankroll and your Edge grow. You should take more risk as your bank. Roll an edge fall, you should take less risk.  No, be on the lookout for the house money effect. When you're up huge on the day, do you trade sloppier? Many people do.  I do systematic increases in Risk, appetite as capital increases are completely different from Winter's tilt and the house money effect. Make sure you understand the difference.  The evolution of expected value over the life of a trade.  There's a particularly tricky Concept in the mathematics of trading. Which is that the risk reward of a given trade changes as prices move.  An example is, if you have a trade where you are, risking 1% to make 2% and then it quickly moves 1.5% in your favor. At that point the question is are you now risking 2 and a half percent to make 0.5% unattractive? Or do you ignore the mark to Market and stick with your original plan? The answer is not obvious, most people respond that you should Mark to Market because the current market price is the only thing that matters. The problem with this approach is that you'll never let your trades, run to the take-profit.  Because the risk reward is so bad. Once the trade moves more than halfway toward the take-profit level,  If you're risking 1 to make 2 but then you always take profit when it moves 1 and a half. Your risk-reward ratio was never 1 to 2. It was 1 to 1.5  even worse. If you get nervous, when it's just moved 1% you take profit and then your trading system is just risking 1 to make 1 decision. For the finish line is before you start the race.  1 way to deal with. This is to remove your stop-loss higher as the trade moves deeper in the money. This is generally logical, but introduces, more noise risk because a random pullback might knock you out when the trade would have been safe under the original parameters.  The most effective way to Trail. Your stop is to use a moving average that matches the trend and update your stop-loss once every 12 or 24 hours, there's no correct answer here. But my general feeling is that if you put a trade on risking 1 to make 2, you cannot take profit all the time. Once you make 1.4 because that means, your original plan, was not really true and your risk reward on every trade is much worse than you think. It is on Inception. You're putting on a trade with a certain plan in mind and then moving the goalposts once the trade moves in the money.  Either Trail your stop-loss higher in a systematic way while still giving the trade plenty of room to breathe or take partial profits at predetermined levels. Trailing the stop systematically breaks. The rule of setting stops based on technical analysis and Market logic, which is not ideal  But you can always fine-tune, whatever stop-loss the system spits out by looking at a few charts. What you should not do, is cut every trade early because quote, the risk reward sucks. Now end quote.  That is a logical fallacy. That ignores the original structure of the trade and ignores, the fact that a core skill and successful trading is the ability to find trades, that offer substantially more reward than risk. Don't truncate your right tail all the time. Trying to extract the last 20% of a trade may cause you to give back all your gains. That's just the way it is. That's just Trading.  As an FYI, this discrepancy between actual payout on trades and forecast payout happens all the time, and it's something you need to monitor. If you're structuring, all your trades, risking 1 to make 3. But then you cut everything early because you get antsy or bored or risk averse, or looking for excuses to reduce Your risk reward is not 1 to 3, might be 1 to 2 or Worse monitor this.  This is frequently an issue in options trading too. Often, you'll see an exotic option like a reverse knockout that pays something like 8 to 1, but the only way for it to pay off that way, is if the option expires exactly 1 basis point away from the barrier, that's totally unrealistic. If you're buying low Delta digital options at 8%, because they look like juicy leverage, but then you sell them every time. Once they double to 16%, you might have been better off trading, a simpler more liquid structure.  If you're structuring trades for maximum leverage, make sure you get the leverage. You thought you were getting  sticking to the plan.  There's always a tension between sticking to the plan and adjusting to new information. The problem with adjusting to new information is that a substantial percentage of the information you received in a given. Trading day is noise, some days are 100% noise, your predisposition, once you have a Trade on should always be to do nothing. Leave it alone and follow your original plan.  Plans are generally made in a rational State of Mind adjustments. On the Fly are often emotional and biased reactions to the non-stop onslaught of stimulus.  Prices are almost always changing. New information is arriving constantly. Most of it is meaningless. If you have a trade on, stick to the original plan, tell there is new information. You cannot possibly ignore  That is assumed new information is mostly noise and react only. When there is an unambiguous signal some Traders, impose a restrictive rule to avoid overtrading. For example, once I put on a trade, I'll reevaluate it once per day at 11:00 a.m. otherwise I'll leave it till the stop or the take-profit trigger.  This avoids, the problem of sitting there staring at the screen as a dumb voices in your head say stuff like it's training, heavy. Maybe I should get out. Ooh, the headlines make me nervous. This trade goes south, I'll be down. On the months look a squirrel.  Know your default mode.  Part of this calculus is knowing your own default mode Network.  The default mode Network or dmn is a cognitive science concept research shows that there's a particular part of your brain that becomes active when you're not busy thinking about anything in particular.  This is also known as wakeful rest.  This area tends to have the same thoughts over and over. These are the thoughts you Circle back to when you're not thinking about anything.  Mendel Kalin suggests. We think of the mind like a hill covered in fresh snow.  Each thought is a sled that slides down that Hill leaving a trail.  The more sleds that slide down those same Trails the deeper those Trails become and the more difficult it becomes to avoid sliding into the same repetitive thought patterns.  What are your default modes?  when there's a trading mode, how do you typically Think what is the action? You default to when you have a trade on and you're sitting in front of the screen, what is your default mode?  Do you generally want to add to the position or cut it? Or are you fine to wait patiently and let it simmer until it's ready?  Become familiar with your default, Dot, and action patterns. And think about whether they are destructive, whether they add to, or subtract from your process, and whether they lead to productive or sub-optimal actions, when you're not busy at work, where does your mind go? Is it a positive or negative place? Are your typical thoughts constructive, or self-critical? Do they encourage action or inaction? Does your dmn help you or hurt you? How do you break the bad habits?  1, part of my default mode is to scan through charts and think about random Market related ideas, that's a positive and productive dmn. I default to thinking things like hmm, vix has dropped 3 days in a row. Is that bullish for stocks or I wonder what? And soybeans are doing. I haven't checked the self- Commodities in a while, or when's the next rbnz meeting?  I also default to tossing around ideas for upcoming editions of AMF, my daily macro newsletter, I add them to a word doc, a future ideas. These are all productive, default mode Network actions,  The negative part of my default mode is a strong and steady urge to press the buy and sell buttons. I always want to put on or add to positions whenever markets, slow down and enter a quieter intraday phase. I find myself tempted to increase my position size.  I should be doing nothing because unnecessary increases in position size often lead to sub-optimal risk management scenarios if price goes the wrong way.  This default mode is such an own part of who I am that my manager, Pete once wrote reduce on a sticky note and stuck it to the bottom right corner of 1, of my monitors.  Anyone who knows? Me has a Trader knows. My positions are either the right size. Or too big almost. Never too small. This is not good.  The reduced sign helped to be maintain awareness of my default mode, which is a subconscious desire to increase risk even when there's no reason to do. So it helps me keep my hands off the buy and sell buttons when there's no logical action to be taken.  Alpha Traders, hold 2 contradictory skills at once they can sit there and do nothing for long periods, and they can act aggressively without hesitation. Most people can do 1, but struggle with the other I'm great at acting with without hesitation. But I'll always be working on my patients. It is also the primary purpose of meditation, and mindfulness exercises. Before we hit chapter 12, let's take a little detour here to talk about Jesse Livermore and the Book reminiscences of a stock operator.  A quick book review kind of thing.  Reminiscences of a stock. Operator, is a quintessential and slightly misunderstood piece of trading literature. This 1923 classic is a slightly fictionalized biography of Jesse Livermore written by Edward, Lefevre.  Livermore was a famous Speculator and Bobby. Axelrod Styles, celebrity rich guy. The book is epic entertainment and full of all kinds of cool, trading, wisdom and Nifty old-timey. Wall Street jargon like tape reading and bucket shops.  It's also a cautionary tale, and a case study in overtrading fed discipline, poor money management, and the Dark Side of trading.  Livermore, often celebrated as a genius and as 1 of the great, speculators of all time went bankrupt, 3 times and eventually Committed suicide in the cloak room of the Sherry Netherland Hotel in New York City.  By all accounts. He was an incredible Trader with fatal flaws.  An interesting fact that tells me he was a classy guy each time. He declared bankruptcy. He would borrow a small stake from a friend so he could start trading again. Then he'd Build It Up substantially eventually making himself Rich again.  Once he was fully liquid, he would pay back all the creditors he owed from before, he declared bankruptcy.  He did this even though he was clear of the debts and was not legally required to repay. Thanks to the bankruptcy proceeding, he just paid people back because it was the right thing to do.  Here are some of my favorite quotes from the book with a bit of my own commentary sprinkled in. The number 1 way, I come up with trade ideas is by surfing the narrative. I explain this fully in chapter 9 I hope you have a sense of what I mean by now.  Surfing the narrative before. You can come up with the trade idea in your Market. You need to be an expert on the narrative that drives. It, this narrative is an evolving story that drives price over time and can often be comprised of 1, major narrative, along with multiple smaller, narratives vying for attention  Rarely Will the Market Embrace 2 major narratives at once that is too complicated. Usually 1 narrative dominates. And the remaining narratives hum in the background waiting to potentially emerge as important drivers.  When you understand the narrative in your Market, you have taken the first and most important step toward becoming an expert Trader. The evolution of the narrative is 1 of the major sources of trade ideas and can be a tremendous help even if you are a very short-term Trader.  For example, if you're a short-term Trader, you cannot possibly trade a new headline unless you fully understand what it means. Let's say your main Market is gold. Its Summer of 2020, you're short. A decent sized position in Gold. Futures risking 3% of your capital, a red headline, hits Bloomberg,  Gold Imports by India, more than doubled to 35.5 tons in August.  What does that mean? Is it bullish or bearish or meaningless? If you're an expert in Gold? The answer may be nobody cares that headlines around.  On the other hand, the market might be worried about Indian demand because so many weddings have been canceled due to co 19.  If speculators were worried that the only demand was institutional and ETF demand, this number might be highly reassuring and could trigger a $10 rally in Gold. You need to know if you're an expert in your Market, you know what matters? And you know, what doesn't, you know, what your Market is trading off, and what it is not and then as the narrative changes, and New information comes in, you can react faster and smarter than others ride the price, to the new equilibrium and get off as it overshoots.  Over time, this knowledge of the narrative will inevitably lead you to trade ideas, you'll feel the winds, change direction before, others and you'll jump on the term before prices move, you may identify a new narrative before others. In trading and in life, the Quest for Perfection is commendable. But the expectation that the world any situation or any trade will be perfect is flawed  You need to know when you reached good enough or you will wait forever for the perfect trade, perfect spouse or perfect job. Perfection is rare and fleeting and anything. Perfect. Should come as a positive. Of surprised not as an expectation.  As the Italian proverb made famous by. Voltar says, perfect, is the enemy of good  This applies to self-evaluation too many Traders, spend way too much time beating themselves up, unless you finish the day at the ding-dong highs in your p&l, you'll always wish you took profit earlier or had a bigger position or did something a little different.  Assess your performance, rationally, set high standards but do not expect Perfection from yourself or every day will be disappointment.  We need to do a good job. Be proud, don't niggle away at small performance glitches when you have generally run well this is draining and pointless.  Once you understand the narrative, you're in a strong position to trade news and events as you will understand their meaning and how they might disrupt or transform, the prevailing story.  News and events.  News and events can be a tremendous source of Edge and trade ideas. If you understand, the current equilibrium, and are familiar with the mind of the market and what information and events led to the current pricing that when new info arrives, you are a ninja events like company, earnings fomc, meetings, other Central Bank, meetings product announcements, economic data and unexpected headlines are opportunities for the fastest most informed traders to capitalize on mispricing  Prices, do not find their happy place right away. When new info arrives, there's a period of fast Market action, as hundreds of humans and algorithms disagree, on fair market, value and trade aggressively against each other.  Until a few winners emerge and trading eventually settles at a new price.  Often these waves of intense trading are when you can enter a state of flow and capture outsized, gains, these moments of In the Zone trading sometimes lasts just 10 or 15 minutes but can account for an entire week of p&l.  Identify events in your Market. Educate yourself. Read every preview and every word of pregame analysis, create a clear plan when the news event or announcement happens attack.  In 2 events flat equals strong. I don't always have the discipline to do this, but I try to go into major economic and Central Bank events as close to Flat as possible. If you're a short-term news Trader, this should be your approach. Here are 3 reasons. It is suboptimal to hold positions into events. 1 Gap risk. Quite often you won't get out as fast as you want, and you'll lose more money than you thought you would. You can't properly control risk in a gaping Market 2. You are biased. If you go into a Fed meeting short dollars because you think they're going to be dovish, your interpretation of the news release and press conference are likely to be biased. You'll be looking for headlines that confirm your prior davish view, instead of looking at the outcome through Clear Eyes  flat is the most powerful position in trading because It is the only time you are fully and completely unbiased 3. It's hard to flip if you're wrong.  If your position for a dovish outcome and the result is hawkish you want to flip from short dollars to Long dollars, this is difficult because 1 you will react slower when you are biased and leaning the wrong way. And 2 liquidity is not unlimited. If you have a decent sized short dollar position, you may have trouble buying enough dollars to flip long at a reasonable price.  It is easier to react to events than it is to predict them as a rule, if you are fast and well, prepared, the Sharpe ratio of reacting to events is higher than the sharp of trying to predict them.  As discussed last chapter, a big part of successful trading is to understand the current price. And then when new information appears react smarter and faster than your competitors, before the market finds a new equilibrium.  If you are flat, when new information comes out, you're stronger, faster and unbiased. You don't always need to have a position. Good Traders. Know when to be flat.  Although, predicting the outcome of an event, often has very little Edge predicting. What the market will do in the days, leading up to the event, can be a good source of trade ideas. Instead of trying to predict what everyone else is predicting the event itself. Smart Traders can often more easily forecast, what the crowd will do into the event and position ahead of the crowd before it starts to move.  The run-up trade.  The run-up trade is a phenomenon where prices move in a logical and predictable Direction. In the days, leading up to a major event.  These run-up trades are often easier to predict and monetize than the events that follow.  The idea behind the run-up trade is to identify an upcoming Market event. And then estimate what Traders will do before the event, if Apple earnings are coming up this week, which way are Traders likely to play it if non-farm payrolls are a few days from now, what might the market do in the days, leading up to The release the run-up trade is usually a function of 3 things.  1 positioning, medium-term. Traders investors and systematic strategies tend to reduce risk into major events.  They do this because volatility tends to be higher and more difficult to predict through events.  If their sizing, their bets using forecast volatility, which most do because that makes sense. They need to reduce their exposure as high ball events near. If everyone's long expect prices to fall in the run-up to the day of the event,  2, what is the obvious trade? Quite often, the obvious trade works. Plus Traders, feel stupid, missing obvious macro catalysts, so they will tend to position for them in advance. Then take them off quickly, if they do not materialize.  If the US economy has been hot. The FED is thinking about hiking rates, and most indicators point to strong job growth. Expect bonds to sell off in the days, leading up to the jobs report. As Traders, get set. For a blockbuster figure.  And 3. What is the lotto ticket trade?  If a biotech company is announcing Phase 1 results on Friday morning and it looks like a good result could cause a stock to Triple while a bad result will cause it to drop 20% expect the stock to Rally into the news. Even if the probability of a positive outcome does not justify the trade from an expected value. Point of view.  Asymmetrical bets will always attract speculators even if they are bad trades with good Optics.  So the run-up trade ahead of an event will be driven by speculators putting on lotto tickets beforehand.  Sometimes, all 3 factors will apply and that's a top quality run-up trade.  When you catch a run-up trade, you'll be tempted to keep it through the event because you have a nice cushion by that point resist that Temptation. It's often easier to forecast The Madness of crowds going into an event than it is to forecast, the outcome of the event itself. Furthermore, The very fact that you cut the run-up trade means, the security has moved a fair bit already into the event and thus, it is probably getting crowded in that direction and offers asymmetric risk reward in a bad way.  Everybody's bearish, but nobody's short.  Sticking with the topic of events. There's a more subtle way that upcoming events can influence markets often when a major risk event is coming up, Risk, Managers and Traders lean on the conservative side until it is out of the way due to Gap risk. And concerns about extreme volatility. They reduce their risk as the event nears, regardless of their View.  Once the event passes regardless of the result risk appetite returns to normal and a move that probably should have happened before the event happens afterwards, speculators put on the trade, they wanted to have before because now that the event and Associated Gap risk is out of the way, it's safe to do so.  This is 1 of my favorite setups. When there's a strong consensus view but no positioning to match the view you often see, highly impulsive moves once the event passes. The consensus move is free to unfold at high speed, often the event itself contains. No new information. It's simply the passing of the event. And thus, the elimination of Gap risk that allows the move that should have happened earlier to happen. Now, this setup is powerful and has a high expected value. So watch for it.  Deviation from fair value.  Another important source of trade ideas is scanning for moments when your Market deviates, from fair value,  Fair value is a slippery concept and is almost impossible to measure with certainty. So when I say fair value, I mean loose or best estimate of fair value,  You never know what the real fair value of a market is. But you know, that if oil drops 2 dollars and copper drops 5% at the same time, fair value for the Canadian dollar has Pro Probably just gone down. Not  Up, I tend to think of fair value, more in terms of Direction and not level. That is, I might say, today's economic data was bad and oils down 4% today. Yields look way too high, but I would never say given these numbers and the movement oil, I think, 10 year yields should be 1.1% not 1.2%.  1 way to measure, deviations from fair, value is to use cross Market correlation. This is explained in chapter 9,  Start by identifying 1 or 2 markets, that should logically be correlated to the market. You trade follow them. You don't need to create a sophisticated regression model to track deviations and movements. Just check momentum in the correlated markets, and get a feel for what it means. For the market, you trade  over time. You'll slowly learn to watch other markets for Clues and you'll experience. How lead lag trading can give you a forecasting Edge.  Lead lag or cross market trading is not just about watching 1 market move. Higher than buying the other Market. It is about putting together a coherent story using fundamental narratives, plus moves and correlated markets. Let's look at an example of how deviation from fair value might lead me not to put on a trade  Let's say I'm an expert in trading, FCX and BHP 2 of the world's largest mining stocks. I'm flat and US markets are closed. But since I'm a highly motivated and curious guy at 9:00 p.m. I flick on Bloomberg TV, to check out the Chinese export and new loans data.  There's a high degree of uncertainty about the direction of the Chinese economy right now. So everyone's watching the release. The data comes out super weak much worse than expected.  Since China's a huge consumer of Commodities this week economic data should be bad news for FCX and BHP. The Market's been trading mining stocks from the long side for months on the back of a popular narrative that China stimulus and strong Chinese growth should boost earnings. The trade is crowded and the narrative looks stale my metrics show. There's a massive long position in Copper so expect it to get hit hard. I care about markets but I also care about health and sleep. So I go to bed at 10 p.m.  I spend the night dreaming about, how much FCX and BHP. I'll go short in the morning and hope that the stocks have not sold off too much in Asia, or London. Time this seems like a solid multi-day, trade idea, with good risk reward but I don't like trading on my phone when us markets are closed. When I wake up, I'm surprised to see FCX and BHP are unchanged. Awesome. My entry point will be amazing here that I flick to Copper expecting it to be down between 2% and 3%, but it's up 1.4%. Huh, that's weird.  The markets had 7 hours to digest, the bad China news. I thought the market was megalong and yet coppers up. 1.4%  At this point, my interest in shorting FCX and BHP would fizzle fast my thesis. Was at the market was long, the whole China narrative, but if that were true copper should be down on the bad news, not up by monitoring other correlated markets. I can get clues about my thesis and filter a bad trade ideas while homing in on good ones.  The better, you understand a suite of related Securities and the underlying fundamental drivers of those Securities, the more trade ideas, you will come up with and the more likely you are to weed out bad ideas before they cost you.  In that example I use deviation from fair, value copper rallying, and BHP and FCX unchanged means BHP and FCX looked low to avoid a bad short.  Let's look at a simple example. A trade like this is not unrealistic for most good trades. I'll have 3 to 5. 5 decent reasons and very few arguments the other way.  This trade is a good example of how cross market analysis. Gives me greater confidence to take the other side of a flow driven move. That is not supported by fundamentals or by action in other markets.  Pattern recognition and indicator scanning.  The final major source of trade ideas, after narrative news and dislocations from fair value is pattern recognition and indicator scanning. This can mean many things and Depends greatly on what products you trade. Good Traders, recognize patterns in their market and understand, which patterns are the result of Randomness and which are sending a persistent signal.  In the section of I discussed the fact that dollar Yen rallies into month end when stocks have done well, that month and we discussed other patterns of activity in various parts of the book.  when I say patterns here, I'm not talking about technical analysis, I'm talking about recurring price patterns, the more you read and study your Market, the more You'll learn about identify and back test. These types of patterns, here are a few examples.  Stocks trade poorly from September 16th, to October 9th, then rip higher into year end.  This seasonal is well known and persistent the worst week of the year for stocks, has been the week after options expire in September, that is the week. After the third Friday of September September 21 to September. 25 is the 5-day window, in which you had the least chance of making money long US stocks from 1990 to 2020.  There's a chart in the PDF and it's pretty dramatic.  I also show in the PDF the average path of equities over the course of the Year, again. Going back to 1990.  There are all sorts of seasonal patterns like this in many markets. You need to be very careful with seasonality because much of it is random experience and statistical Acumen can help you sort through what is random and what is persistent.  Analogs. Analogs bolster your understanding of narratives and volatility.  another common pattern is that bubbles tend to Deflate by about 85% before rebounding looking at the major bubbles in recent history. Another pattern is that highs and lows are more likely on round numbers than on other numbers. I explained this in the section on round number bias. After beginning, bankruptcy proceedings, it can take months or even years before the stock is zeroed out.  It's not worth it for speculators to stay short all that time when there is. So little juice left in the trade  this is kind of a ridiculous trade, given your buying the shares of a bankruptcy, but it doesn't always have to make sense, often buying the shares of a bankrupt company, when the stock reopens, after the halt, for the bankruptcy, announcement can be highly profitable as a short-term 1 day to 1 week trade,  the patterns I've described are just the tip of the iceberg as you gain, expertise in your market and watch markets in general over the years  You'll discover more and more patterns. Remember that if you can explain a pattern and why it should continue to repeat, there is a much Higher likelihood. That pattern will be useful for trading.  Random patterns that you observe or discover by data mining are quite often simply the inevitable result of the fact that random data is chocked full of mostly meaningless patterns.  Find patterns and do your best to explain them, then make a rational determination of whether they might repeat in the future.  Indicator scanning.  Indicator scanning is a process where you have specific filters that will identify trading opportunities. This might be volume more large move filters in the equity Market, or a dashboard that shows abnormal moves in options markets,  Scanning for large moves and unusual activity can often lead you in the direction of a good idea.  The markets are mostly a sea of efficient tranquility and you're looking for whirlpools and weirdness. That might tell you something abnormal is happening. We discussed this in depth in chapter 5, and I mentioned, Richard's Huer of the CIA and his 8 step process. I said it then and I'll say it again here, you should read that book. You not just in trading, but in any area of life that benefits from rational thought,  At this stage of the trade idea process, you need to think about the other side. If your trade idea is bearish corn, Futures. Think about what the Bulls are saying, find research notes that are bullish, go through their arguments, can you debunk them?  Sometimes I read counterarguments to my view, and I think, Ah, that's pretty unconvincing. So, I remain confident in my view. Sometimes I read the counterarguments and think, oh, crap, I totally missed that.  Remember that your brain is programmed to find supporting evidence that confirms your existing up? Hypothesis. You need to work hard to fight against that programming and honestly, pursue alternative stories. That might make sense.  A useful tool when attempting to embrace alternative hypothesis is to picture how the trade could go against you.  What would that look like, what specific factors or events might derail your idea and stop you out? This helps. You think about your idea from different angles and hopefully with greater objectivity and also gets you thinking about possible reassessment triggers if I'm wrong. What's it going to look like? This is something called a pre-mortem as discussed in Chapter 7.  Found your trade idea of people. You respect and see what comes back. Don't blindly defer to them or hope. But you remain confident about it, that can be powerful because now, you know, you are not part of the Consensus. You have rigorously tested, your idea and it is resilient to scrutiny.  When someone smart gives me a good reason to doubt my idea. And I can't really refute their argument. I'm happy to toss my trade idea in the trash and wait, for a better 1 to come along.  Don't hold on to your ideas too tightly. This goes back to the idea of strong opinions, weakly held, which I discussed earlier. You need to come up with strong trade ideas, backed, by significant research, and multiple streams of analysis. But then have an open mind, admit error and change course of need be as new information comes in.  This is easy to say and hard to do.  strong opinions that are a 2 weekly held will be scared off by the first bit of noise, determining when to give up on a trade, is tough, we'll deal with that a bit later in this chapter,  3 tactics.  Okay, after filtering is done, it's game on your idea is good, and now it's time to determine tactics. Don't rush to your keyboard and start pressing the buy or sell buttons yet. First, it's time to fine-tune the tactics to maximize leverage and make the most money possible if you are right, being right. And making money are not always the same thing, you can be right and get stopped out because of a poorly, chosen stop-loss level, you can buy an option that expires worthless, the day before your beloved asset, rips to the Moon,  You can enter a trade mid-range make 11 Bibb, when the guy next to you threads. The needle at Major support makes 111 bips being, right? Is just the start.  Before you can put on your trade idea. You need to decide what to trade in many cases. This is obvious but not always. For example, I often have ideas driven by stock market views, but due to the nature of my role, I can't trade stocks. Therefore once I come up with a view on where stocks are going, I need to figure out what the best FX proxy is for stocks often. That means Aussie Yen or Diane, but sometimes it might mean Aussie dollar Color or even Euro Swiss.  I analyze 90-day correlation stats and think about what makes sense going forward. Then finally pick what product to trade,  If your Bears, the NASDAQ, you need to decide between QQQ sqqq qid, psq or NASDAQ futures or other ideas, you might even decide that a specific subgroup short, Fang via fngd, makes sense. Or you might take a view in a single name, like Amazon or Nvidia,  Which choice will give you the exposure, leverage, and directional movement you want with the lowest transaction costs.  Be thoughtful about the product, you choose to express your view, it matters.  Once you've chosen your product, it's time to pick your entry point.  Stop loss and take profit. The first thing I do here is pull up a chart. What's the dream entry point? What is a realistic entry point is the exact place where the market is trading. Right now, the best place to get in or am I better off waiting?  Obvious. This will often come down to the type of trade it is. If you're trading news, you don't have time to wait for the ideal entry point. You get in and then you determine your tactics after. But if this is a scenario where you're playing a turn in narrative and your trade is likely to last 3 to 5 days, you can finesse your entry point.  Using technical analysis. I will determine the ideal entry and stop loss at this point.  Rarely will I have a stop-loss closer than 1 day's range away unless the trade is ultra short-term, while a risk reward ratio of 1 to 2 or 1 to 3 is a decent rule of thumb for most trades.  There's no hard and fast rule. If you think there's a 70% chance, you can risk 1 to make 1 and you have high confidence in your 70%, probability estimate, do the trade, it has high expected value. On the other hand, given the significant uncertainty around probabilities and trading higher payout, ratios, give you a wider margin of safety.  Solid tactics. Give you Superior leverage because the more Or fine-tuned your entry point and the tighter your stop-loss, the larger position, you can take with the same amount of money at risk.  If you buy Apple at 100 with a stop loss at 99, you can take 5 times bigger position than if you buy Apple at 100 with a stop at 95.  Then again, your odds of getting stopped out at 99 are clearly higher than getting stopped out at 95. So choosing a stop-loss is always about the tension between as tight as possible and lowest chance of getting stopped out.  If you want to assist in that is simple. And generally effective start by risking 1 day's range to make 2. Or if your day trading risk 35% of an average day's range to make 70% of a day's range.  Those are finger in the air starting points and you can adjust as you gain experience.  When things are going well, and you have a strong feeling for your Market, you'll sometimes find trades where you can risk much less to make much more. Sometimes you can sell right against major resistance and risk just 20 % of a day's range knowing that if you catch the turn, it might be a major top and you can extract 2 full days, ranges from the trade, those trades are dreamy. Each trade will have its own ideal parameters based on technical analysis and the unique timing features of the trade.  tactics are just as important as strategy, you need both to succeed in trading  Once you have your entry stop loss and take profit determined automate everything. As much as possible, if Apple's at 100 and you've determined that 10150 is the perfect entry point for the limit order in the system, along with your stop-loss. If you don't put in a system right away, what do you think is going to happen in 2 hours when Apple spikes from 100 to 10150 your dumb? Lizard brain will say stuff like whoa it looks good. I better check if there's some news out or hmm. Maybe I can sell it 103 and get a better entry point or somebody must know something. Why is it rallying so hard?  Selling into a rally. Can be hard, just like buying dips as hard. Much easier is to calmly. Enter a limit order to sell at 10150 when nothing's going on and the stock is bouncing around near 100.  Automate and Outsource as much of the execution process as you can and you'll find you get better results, no matter how disciplined you are computers are more disciplined than you.  The last stage in the tactics phase is to write down or record, your list of reassessment triggers a Trader who clearly lays out. Her plan is way ahead of most Traders. She's cemented a concrete process and listed specific triggers for reassessment or exit.  Reassessment triggers are important because once you have a trade on, you'll be influenced by all sorts of new information that is mostly noise numbers jiggle on a screen, random headline, scroll by your heart rate ticks up.  Those are not reasons to adjust your risk. It's easier to list action triggers xanti than to hope you'll be saying. And unbiased once the trade starts to move Reassessment triggers will always include a stop loss and a take profit but can also be other things such as 1 time trigger. For example, any day trade can have a reassessment or firm exit time of 4 p.m. when the main New York Stock Exchange trading session ends.  Many good trades. Have a Time trigger. Another example, say, you've done a ton of work on today's US, retail sales number and you expect it will be weak.  You go long bonds at 7:45 a.m. in anticipation of the release at 8:30 a.m.  You believe that most of the new information from economic data gets fully priced within 10 minutes. Does it make any sense to keep the trade past 8:45 or maybe 9:00 a.m. at the latest? Probably not. So you put a reassessment trigger at 8:45 a.m.  2 level at which, to tighten the stop loss. Once a trade is made most of its gains. It makes sense to tighten up the stop loss.  3 new information trigger. Need to be as specific as possible here to avoid. Undue reaction to noise. If a Trader gets long oil on a Saudi Arabia, Russia squabble the trader. Should logically have a reassessment trigger as truce or consili Saudi Arabia and Russian headlines.  That is a logical reassessment trigger.  A bad reassessment trigger would be something like re-evaluate if price action does not cooperate, be specific.  And for cross Market variable trigger, if your long S&P futures and 1 of your main reasons for entering the trade, is that the vix just dropped below the key, 20 Level, it makes sense to use the vix as a potential cross Market reassessment trigger again, make it specific for example, if vix trades back above 21, I will cut my position.  Note that I'm using the term reassessment triggers, but often these are action triggers. The last example, is an action trigger. I use reassessment trigger and action trigger interchangeably. Since often a reassessment leads to an action, The first 2 re assessment triggers for any trade are always the stop-loss and take-profit after that, you record. Anything else that might change your mind, the goal for this stage of the process is to try to guess xante, what might be signal and what might be noise going forward.  When you have a trade on every price movement, feels like a signal or a sign of danger for your position. While in reality most price movement is noise.  every time price Jiggles lower or ratchets higher, you'll have an emotional reaction because you're invested the idea with reassessment triggers is to codify what will make you change your mind and what will not before the price starts flickering and jiggling around, and those dumb voices in your head, start to react to that flickering and jiggling,  Some other examples of good reassessment triggers.  A long stocks because I think the FED has turned more devish and the market hasn't caught on yet. There are 2 fed speakers this week. If either 1 of those speakers is more hawkish than I expect. I'll reassess my Or I'm sure bonds because I think the economy is strengthening any major US data point that comes in more than 1 standard deviation below consensus will trigger a reassessment.  Or.  1 of my main reasons for going long Aussie dollar was the rebounding copper and iron ore prices. If either copper or iron ore break below their 200 hour moving average, I'll reassess my Aussie dollar long position  If you take the time to record these reassessment triggers, you should find very few occasions where you make on the fly to decisions made trade. Once you have a trade on, leave it alone and let it bake. Don't rush it, don't keep opening the oven to Peak the market doesn't care about your time frame. A market that is not moving is boring but it's not telling you anything. Wait for your stop-loss your take profit or 1 of your reassessment triggers to hit stop white knuckling. Of course, there are going to be times when you're long and bad news comes out and you hit a bin and you save yourself some cash but these are not all that. Common just, as often, you'll hit the bid and find yourself, buying the stock back, 10 minutes later after realizing the news wasn't, as bad as you thought. And in fact, the news was actually meaningless. Remember that when you have a trade on you are biased. Information processing is distorted by your emotional state.  The best action is almost always no action. This is true when you have a position on and it's also true. Most of the time when you don't  Let me your actions as much as possible and you will put on only the best trades.  Then you'll stay in those trades long enough for them to bear fruit. This Is My Kryptonite and the greatest strengths and greatest weakness in my life. I can do a thousand push-ups in a day every Wednesday for 10th, straight weeks action but have an incredibly tough time. Going 2 hours with that touching the buyer or sell buttons in action.  This bias to action, gives me the energy to write a decent macro newsletter every day, and to pump out a book every year or 2, but it also makes it shockingly difficult for me to sit at work. And keep my hands off the trading controls.  My default mode is action. I've known that for 20 years and yet is still my greatest weakness in trading. I never stopped working on it, but I also accept it is who I am. I forgive myself more easily now than I did when I was 30. I try to automate as much as possible and convince myself to sit there and do nothing as much as possible.  Once, you know, your personal Kryptonite, fine-tune your process and redesign your framework to address it.  4 position sizing.  Position size is a function of 6 factors 1 convention level 2 free Capital 3 year to date, pno 4, Market volatility, 5, distance, from entry point to stop-loss and 6, Gap and liquidity risk.  Here's a quick summary of how to determine position size.  A toad on your conviction level.  B given your year to date, p&l and free capital. Decide on a percentage of your Capital to put at risk. This tells you how many dollars or basis points, you'll be risking on the trade C, using Market volatility and technical analysis. Choose the appropriate stop-loss D. Calculate the position size, which is dollars at risk, divided by risk per unit to get the total number of units.  E, think about liquidity, is this a reasonable position size? Can you get in and out with that much of an issue?  If not, can you scale in and out and maintain the economics of the trade?  And F think about Gap risks. What major events are coming up, how is the product been trading? Do you need to worry about limit up and limit down? Is there enough liquidity for this position size? In this step? You might reduce your position size because of a gap risk or consider exiting the trade before an upcoming event.  I'll go through this process again in the trade. Walkthrough at the end of this chapter, that should solidify your understanding.  Step 5. The trade process is execution. Good execution, reduces transaction costs and improves returns. This is important at all skill levels and in all trading businesses. Remember those charts that showed the impact of transaction costs in chapter 8,  transaction costs are important. It's lazy to ignore them or minimize their importance. Take them seriously.  Here are a few ways to reduce transaction costs and pay. Less bid, ask spread.  I discuss these approaches from the point of view of an equity Trader that these Concepts apply to trading in most markets,  1 use limit orders if Tesla's trading 281.10 bid and 281.3 offered and you want to buy, you have some choices. You can.  A send a market order and get filled around. 28130.  B, leave a limit order at Mid that is buy or sell at 28120 or C, leave a limit order to buy just ahead of The best bid or sell, just ahead of the best offer.  The decision depends mostly on the level of urgency, you feel option? A gets you filled every time and is the way to go. If there's news, if it's a fast Market, or if you're stopping out of a position,  Option, b is less likely to get filled, but still has a high probability of execution. The risk is that you bid 28120 and the market goes straight up in that case, you will not get filled. Then, again, if you bid or offer at Mid and you will get filled, the majority of the time in most markets, if you show a bit of patience,  Option C saves you the most on transaction cost, but can be riskiest since you need to wait for someone to cross a full spread. This can be a good strategy when nothing's going on but you need to weigh the risk of the trade. Going hard in your direction before you get filled.  I think people use way too many Market orders and not enough limit orders, but I've seen a change in FX markets over the past 5 years as more clients now use limits.  some people feel that when you leave a limit or, Order you give a free option to market makers or high frequency Traders, and it is true that limit orders, have some value and optionality for banks and hfts.  Still the money you save by strategically using limit orders, to minimize your transaction costs is worth it.  Crossing spreads with Market orders all the time is a huge leak for most Traders.  No order type or execution style is optimal at all times. It depends on the market and the situation in a product with a wideband offer spread, you're more likely to miss the trade if you don't agree but you'll also be saving more by using a passive strategy comes down to your view of what's going to happen in the very short term.  If you're worried about the trade ripping in a straight line before you can get it on with a limit order, use a market order. If you think nothing's going on or the market might even move against you a bit before the idea Works, use a limit order or algorithm to trade.  when in doubt consult an expert or Market maker in the product you trade to get a An idea of liquidity, it offer bounce and what might be the weak side in the short term.  Another way to save on transactional costs, if you are a large, institutional Trader is don't stick to 1 execution style. All the time, learn about the different order types, and algorithms you have at your disposal and mix in various methods, depending on your urgency and on market conditions talked to a few market makers you trust and ask them, how they transact there's a time and a place for Taps. But there's also a time and a place to pick up the phone and do a risk transfer.  Execution, strategy, always comes down to the trade-off between footprint and Market risk.  Fast aggressive execution has a larger footprint but less Market risk. If you go slow, you pay less at transaction costs. But you take the risk, the market will move against you in the meantime.  When it is time to execute, you need to decide how to balance the tension between minimizing footprint and Market risk at the same time.  Generally, for larger. Trades. I find a hybrid of human and electronic or algorithmic. Execution is best. You write a few algorithms in the background to take care of half your size and make sure the trade won't take all day and you run the other half with discretion using limit orders, while absorbing any meaningful chunks of liquidity, more aggressively  1 weakness with twap and vwap orders is that they generally ignore the amount of liquidity available in the market. Let's say euro dollar is 1 15 000 and a client wants to buy 500 million euros. They decide to run a 50-minute tap. That is by 10 million euros per minute for 50 minutes.  The computer starts buying drip, drip, drip, drip the computer buy zeros slowly, but surely at 1:15, 01, and 1502.  After 3 minutes a large Cellar appears.  Someone shows a large limit order and offered to sell 250 million euros at 1:52.  a rational human Trader would almost always By those 250 million euros because that's a huge chunk of the order to fail. Just 1 pip off the bid on the other hand, a t won't care, it'll just keep buying 10 euros per minute even though it could snag a huge clip of Euros. All at once, if it was smarter,  A hybrid algo, human strategy, gives you the best of both worlds.  A third way to think about transaction costs is to ask are there Securities or products to trade within your asset class that have lower transaction costs.  Many products trade as cash over the counter future ETFs and so on ETFs are great for some Traders. But Futures often have tighter bid offer spreads with the identical exposure.  For example, you might be better off trading S&P, futures, not the Spy ETF when you have the choice, Futures also offer more leverage than ETFs.  If you have a longer holding period, you might also discover that features are cheaper to hold than ETFs because Futures do not charge a management fee while ETFs do.  For example. SLV. The silver ETF charges 0.65% per year.  This is not an epic fee by any means, but if you can trade, silver Futures, you might find that more attractive depending on funding costs. I'm not telling you which products are best. I'm simply saying you should take the time to figure out the best way to trade your product, if there are various Expressions available.  Currencies trade over the counter but it's not always efficient for a hedge fund to set up credit with a bank. Sometimes it's cheaper for a fund to trade currency, Futures option. It's better for smaller participants to execute through an online FX broker. Sometimes they'd be better off in features. Point here is to think about the various ways of trading your asset or Market. If more than 1 Avenue exists,  And a fourth consideration with regard to transaction costs. When you trade a particular asset, ask yourself, whether there's another asset or security within your asset class that will give you the same exposure, with better liquidity for For example, a hedge fund PM who manages 1.4 billion expects China growth to pick up in the next few months.  He decides to go long, 500 million New Zealand Dollars ensured, the US dollar,  Given Aussie dollar is substantially more liquid than New Zealand dollar. Would he have been better off just buying Aussie? Very likely the answer in this scenario is yes.  Sticking with FX markets. Sometimes a basket will also give you the same exposure with better liquidity and lower transaction costs.  If a Speculator wants to take a huge bet on a falling dollar, she might be better off to execute 20% of her risk across a basket of 5 different currencies. Instead of going 100% short, say dollar Swiss  This situation applies to credit too where a basket of names can often give you bigger exposure with lower transaction costs than piling into 1 single name.  Retail Traders rarely have to worry about liquidity. And this is a huge Advantage for small Traders. They can trade anything. Whatever it's open and never have to worry about the liquidity. Yes, they need to worry about transaction costs but they don't have to worry about being able to buy or sell when they want or need to  I've heard retail Traders complain that If Only They had a bigger account, they could succeed in trading. That is almost always false. It's like, saying I can't win this race on a jet ski. If only I had an aircraft carrier.  A larger account is more difficult to trade especially now that almost every asset class has minis or fractional shares or unlimited divisibility like crypto.  The only Thomas small account is a handicap is. If you need to make X money and your account size is too small to do that. Generally smaller accounts can achieve more leverage, pay lower transaction costs. Have more assets to choose from and can achieve higher percentage returns.  in contrast, when funds grow larger than about 1 billion, the list of products they can trade slowly shrinks small caps and thinly traded credits or some short dated options, for example, become impossible to trade when you Here in behemoth.  Smart execution, improves performance.  Once you've executed your trade, automate, your stop loss and your take profit. If you can and record the trade in your Journal. These final steps should always be part of your execution process.  Here are a few more thoughts on stop losses.  1, if you trade huge size, it is more difficult to automate your stops. This is a difficult fact of life for very large Traders, but does not apply to most even a stop for 100 million Euros, which is a decent position size and FX is perfectly manageable.  If I'm trading a position, that's too large to automate. My stop loss. I simply automate whatever percentage I can.  If I'm short, 200 million, Aussie dollar, and I think anything about 50 million Aussies, too much to automate, I'll put a stop for 50 million in my system. At least that's a placeholder and serves as an alert. When my level triggers once, the 50 million has been purchased automatically, I'll get busy and cover the rest of the position ASAP. This is less than ideal since it relies on discipline and can lead to numb brain comments. Like, let me wait to see if it pulls back. Maybe I can get these on the bid or man Aussie, rallied so much already. I can't pay way up here.  When your stop loss level hits, you must act to get out of the position so you can survive to play again tomorrow. Don't screw around, trying to get the best possible execution. That's what you do on the way in not on the way out.  2, Sell stops should always be below around number and buy. Stop, should always be above around number. This is because of round number by bias. If you ever see a Trader leave, a buy stop in a stock at 999.99 or 100 dollars. Exactly. That Trader probably doesn't know what he's doing experts. Always leave their stops on the correct side of the round numbers.  3.  If your stop loss is in a really obvious place, where a ton of stops are likely to be, it's probably going to get done.  you need a bit of second level thinking when placing stop losses, if there's a triple bottom at 101, And everyone in the market is talking about it. Expect, there will be a pocket of stop losses at $100.80. Leave yourself below, $100. Instead the extra space will protect you from a whipsaw through 101 and could be the difference between a winning and a losing trade.  And finally, on stops when deciding between 2, stop loss levels, pick the wider. Stop surviving the trade is more important than maximizing leverage, though. Obviously, there's always an important tension between those 2 goals when you get stopped out at the lows before a huge rally. It really hurts.  Step 6 in the trading process is tracking. New information comes in  stick to the plan, when the Reward has changed substantially considered, tightening up your stop-loss.  And step 7 in the process is performance analysis.  High-performing trading professionals, constantly, iterate through a process of plan. Execute analyze improve plan, execute analyze improve.  It's a never-ending cycle of action and Analysis that should deal better and better results as errors are corrected and strengths are leveraged.  The information and feedback from your details trade records and Analysis will help improve your process.  Each month that you trade, your process gets a bit more solid and you use these improvements to plan better and trade, more successfully.  The time to analyze performance is when you are well, rested and calm.  After you exit a trade record, the information in your trading journal with as much detail as possible.  Your journal entry should emphasize the decision-making process once a month. Go through your journal, to look for patterns. Are you sticking to your player? Do you take profit halfway to your Target because you get nervous. Do you move your stop loss for no reason or ignore it sometimes?  Compare your training statistics to your history. Are you improving or getting worse? Focus specifically on the size of your winning days? Versus the size of your losing days? This is a key metric. If it's getting better or worse. Can you explain why?  The number 1 question, you need to ask is what is working and what is not.  Search for patterns and adjust your strategy and tactics to eliminate leaks and repeated errors. Are you biased to a particular direction that is? Are you always short? S&P futures and never long?  If you yes, either stop trading that product or open your mind to both sides. Directional. Bias is a huge weakness for short-term Traders.  once you complete your analysis and tweak your process, stop thinking about trades in the past and go back to thinking forward, what I mean by this is Ring fence your performance analysis. Do it all at once. Then, move on. Don't keep thinking about trades. You did in the past. Stay flexible and Achieve long-term trading success.  Quote adopt. Or perish. Now, as ever is Nature's inexorable imperative end quote.  HG Wells.  Trading success can be fleeting or sustained. In this chapter, we discuss the mindset and approach that will allow you to thrive and survive as a Trader over the course of years and then decades.  Latching on to 1 style or overcoming to a specific methodology is not the answer. The answer is adaptation and flexibility.  Excellent Traders adapt to changes in market conditions and micro structure over time, Alpha Traders recognized, for example, that Trend following was delivering, lower and lower returns in the 1990s and of complaining and sticking with the failing strategy, they developed new strategies, and found new ways to trade in the 2000s.  When they saw algorithms enter the market in the mid-2000s, they did not scream. These Algos are stupid. This is dumb. Instead they thought, okay, this is a major To change your Market structure. How am I going to adapt? How can I beat the Algos? How do I make money in this new regime?  No Market regime lasts forever, profitable strategies slowly disappear because efficient markets discover them and crowd into them.  If everyone is making money trading correlation, you can bet it will attract more participants with more participants profit shrink and strategies die.  You saw this with the high frequency firms. In the 2010s, initially, high frequency Traders, were oboli profitable across the board, but as the decade wore on the arms race heated up faster and smarter strategies, and by the end of the 2010s, most high frequency strategies, barely generated enough returns to cover costs  A small group of Highly adaptable. If you can size dynamically using forward-looking estimates of volatility that's ideal. Look at what options markets are pricing for 1 week volatility, if you can't do that, look at the average daily rate. Over the past 5 10 or 20 days.  As a rough logic, check for day Traders, your stop loss should rarely or never be closer than within 1/3 of a day's range.  For swing Traders, use 1 full, day's range. In other words, if you're trading Apple common stock and the average daily range over the past 5 10, and 20 days is 25 bucks. Day trader stops should be 8 or more away from entry and swing Trader. Stop should be, at least twenty 5 dollars from entry. This should be a good starting point in most markets.  If you get stopped out and chopped up every day, your stops are too tight. A smaller position with a wider. Stop is necessary in crisis markets, but you need to be mindful that you don't get so small, you're trading, meaningless positions, that won't move the needle on your pnl.
